contribution. We also obtain
|\sigma|=\sqrt{\alpha}+O(x)
. Moreover, both
\sigma
and
s
are small and
x\geq 0
, so that (
2.72
) implies that
|\sigma|,|s|\leq\sqrt{\alpha}
for the phase of
V_{N}
to be stationary in
\sigma,s
; for
|\sigma|,|s|\geq 2\sqrt{\alpha}
we can apply the non-stationary phase theorem, and from (
2.69
), for
T_{0}
sufficiently small such that (
2.67
) to hold, the phase of
V_{N}(t,x,y)
is stationary in
\alpha
only for

(2.74)

2|N|\sqrt{\alpha}\leq|t|+|\sigma|+|s|\leq|t|+2\sqrt{\alpha}\,.

We used again here that for
T_{0}
sufficiently small and by finite speed of propagation, the coefficient of
\partial_{\alpha}{\tau_{q}}
in the lefthand side of (
2.69
) is
t+y(O(y)+O(a))\sim t
and that
\partial_{\alpha}{\tau_{q}}\sim 1/2
. Moreover, for values
2|N|\sqrt{\alpha}\geq 2|t|+4\sqrt{\alpha}
, non stationary phase in
\alpha
provides an
O(h^{\infty})
contribution from all
V_{N}
with
|N|\geq 2+|t|/\sqrt{\alpha}
. As both
a\lesssim\alpha
and the number of
V_{N}
is (large but) finite (
|N|\leq h^{-1/3}
), non-trivial contributions in
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)
may only be provided by the sum over
|N|\lesssim|t|/\sqrt{\alpha}\lesssim|t|/\sqrt{a}
. We have thus proved the second point in Proposition
2.14
.

Finally, we turn to the data: by design,
V_{0}(0,x,y)=\chi_{0}(hD_{x}){\varkappa}(hD_{y})\delta_{(a,0)}+O_{C^{\infty}}(h^{\infty})
, and we are left to proving that, for
0<|N|\lesssim h^{-1/3}
,
V_{N}(0,x,y)\in O_{C^{\infty}}(h^{\infty})
. In this part we consider
g_{h,a}
as provided by Lemma
2.12
, of the form (
2.38
)
with phase function
\psi_{a}({\varrho},\theta^{\prime})={\varrho}^{3}/3+a({\varrho}q^{1/3}(\theta^{\prime})+O({\varrho}^{3}))+O(a^{2})\,
.
Then
K_{\omega}(g_{h,a})
is of the form (
2.33
) with
f
replaced by
g_{h,a}
, that we re-write here

(2.75)

K_{\omega}(g_{h,a})(t,x,y)=\frac{h^{2/3}}{2\pi h^{d}}\int e^{\frac{i}{h}(t{\tau_{q}}(h^{2/3}\omega,\theta)+\Phi(x,y,\theta,h^{2/3}\omega,\sigma)-y^{\prime}\cdot\theta-{\varrho}h^{2/3}\omega)}p_{h}(x,y,\theta,h^{2/3}\omega,\sigma)q^{1/6}(\theta)\\
\times h^{-d}e^{\frac{i}{h}(\psi_{a}({\varrho},\theta^{\prime})+y^{\prime}\cdot\theta^{\prime})}r_{h}(\varrho,\theta^{\prime})q^{-1/6}(\theta^{\prime})\,d\theta^{\prime}\,dy^{\prime}d{\varrho}d\theta d\sigma\,.

For all
N
,
V_{N}
is an oscillatory
integral, that we rewrite, using (
2.54
) and Proposition
2.14
,

(2.76)

\displaystyle V_{N}(t,x,y)=

\displaystyle h^{-2/3}\int e^{-iNL(h^{-2/3}\alpha)}{\chi^{\flat}}(\alpha/\epsilon_{0}){\chi}^{\sharp}(4\alpha/a)K_{h^{-2/3}\alpha}(g_{h,a})(t,x,y)d\alpha

(2.77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi h^{d}}\int e^{-iNL(h^{-2/3}\alpha)}{\chi^{\flat}}(\alpha/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(4\alpha/a)e^{\frac{i}{h}(t{\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta)+\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)-y^{\prime}\cdot\theta-{\varrho}\alpha)}

\displaystyle\times p_{h}(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)q^{1/6}(\theta)h^{-d}e^{\frac{i}{h}(\psi_{a}({\varrho},\theta^{\prime})+y^{\prime}\cdot\theta^{\prime})}r_{h}(\varrho,\theta^{\prime})q^{-1/6}(\theta^{\prime})\,d\theta^{\prime}\,dy^{\prime}d{\varrho}d\theta d\sigma\,.

We can write
q_{h}:={\chi^{\flat}}(\alpha/\epsilon_{0}){\chi^{\sharp}}(4\alpha/a)p_{h}r_{h}
, which is an elliptic symbol of order
0
; indeed, recall that
p_{h}
comes with a factor
h^{-1/3}
while
r_{h}
comes with a factor
h^{1/3}
. At
t=0
, the stationary points of
V_{N}
with respect to
\alpha,s,\varrho,y^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\theta
are solutions to the following equations

(2.78)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{\alpha}\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=\varrho+2N\alpha^{1/2}\Big(1-\frac{3}{4}B^{\prime}_{L}(\alpha^{3/2}/h)\Big)\,,\\
\sigma^{2}+xq^{1/3}(\theta)+{\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta)x\partial_{\sigma}A_{\Gamma}(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/{\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta),\theta/{\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta))=\alpha\,,\\
\partial_{\varrho}\psi_{a}(\varrho,\theta^{\prime})=\alpha\,,\\
\theta^{\prime}=\theta\,,\,\,\,\,\nabla_{\theta^{\prime}}\psi_{a}(\varrho,\theta^{\prime})=y^{\prime}\,,\,\,\,\,\nabla_{\theta}\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=y^{\prime}\,.\end{array}\right.

From the first two equations we get (
2.74
) (at
t=0
), which allows to conclude that if
|N|\geq 2
the phase is non-stationary in
\alpha
. Hence we are left with
|N|=1
.
The equations from the last line in (
2.78
) give
\nabla_{\theta}\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=\nabla_{\theta}\psi_{a}(\varrho,\theta)
and therefore

(2.79)

a\varrho\nabla_{\theta}(q^{1/3}(\theta))+O(a\varrho^{2},a^{2})=y+\sigma x\nabla_{\theta}(q^{1/3}(\theta))+\nabla_{\theta}({\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta)\Gamma).

For
{\tau_{q}}={\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta)
we have
\nabla_{\theta}{\tau_{q}}=(\theta+\alpha\frac{\nabla_{\theta}(q^{1/3}(\theta))}{3q^{1/3}(\theta)})/{\tau_{q}}
and

(2.80)

\nabla_{\theta}\Big({\tau_{q}}\Gamma(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/{\tau_{q}},\theta/{\tau_{q}})\Big)=(\nabla_{\theta}{\tau_{q}})\Gamma(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/{\tau_{q}},\theta/{\tau_{q}})\\
{}+{\tau_{q}}\Big(\nabla_{\theta}(B_{\Gamma}(y,\theta/{\tau_{q}}))+x\nabla_{\theta}(A_{\Gamma}(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/{\tau_{q}},\theta/{\tau_{q}}))\Big).

From
B_{0}=O(|y|^{2})
,
B_{2}=O(|y|^{2})
,
B_{2j}=O(|y|)
we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}({\tau_{q}}(\alpha,\theta)\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}{\tau_{q}}\Big(O(y^{3})+O(y^{2}\alpha)+O(y\alpha^{2})+x(O(\sigma)+O(\alpha))\Big)+{\tau_{q}}(O(y\alpha)+xO(\sigma))

\displaystyle=O(y^{3})+O(y\alpha)+O(x\sigma)\,.

Combining this with (
2.79
) yields
y=O(a\varrho)+O(x\sigma)+O(a^{2})
, at the stationary phase points
y^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},\theta
of
V_{N}
. Rescale
x=aX
,
\alpha=aq^{1/3}(\theta)\mathcal{A}
,
\sigma=\sqrt{a}q(\theta)^{1/6}\mathcal{S}
and
\varrho=\sqrt{a}q(\theta)^{1/6}\mathcal{T}
; from the first three equations of (
2.78
) (and
\theta^{\prime}=\theta
) we get

(2.81)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{T}+2{\mathcal{A}}^{\frac{1}{2}}=O(a^{1/2})\,,\quad\mathcal{S}^{2}+(1+\ell)X+O(a^{1/2})={\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{T}}^{2}+1+O(a^{1/2})\,.

As
a\leq a_{0}
is small (taking
a_{0}
even smaller if necessary),
\mathcal{T}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}-\frac{1}{2}
; then, using that
\ell=O(y)=O(a^{3/2})
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for
X\geq-\varepsilon
,
\mathcal{S}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}+\frac{1}{10}
. For these values, the first equation cannot hold and non stationary phase in
\alpha
allows to conclude. Again, that we can integrate by parts in
V_{\pm 1}(0,x,y)
relies on
a\geq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
.
∎

2.3.
Parametrix construction for
a<h^{2/3-\epsilon}
,
0<\epsilon<1/12

In formulas (
2.49
) and (
2.50
),
G(\cdots,\omega)
can be written in terms of the Airy function
Ai(-\zeta)
and its derivative, as stated in Theorem
2.1
. Using the explicit form of
\zeta
, for
\zeta<0
(hence for
\omega\leq a/(4h^{2/3})
), these Airy factors in
G
are exponentially decreasing, so the main contribution comes from values
a\lesssim 4\omega h^{2/3}
. For
a\gtrsim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, this implies
\omega\gtrsim h^{-\epsilon}
and one may perform stationary phase arguments in the integrals from (
2.49
), as long as we pick any
\epsilon>0
. In this section,
a
is much smaller and very different issues arise compared to the previous one; we need a different way to construct a suitable
g_{h,a}
to recover the initial data. The explicit upper bound on
\epsilon
will be of use in this section and later required in Section
4
; there is quite an overlap between both parametrix constructions for
h^{2/3}<a<h^{2/3-1/12}
, but we made not attempt at enlarging it; the reader is advised to think
\epsilon
to be really small.

Besides the lack of a large parameter, which forces us to work with (
2.50
), the regime
a<h
has its own difficulties: even deciding how the initial data should be chosen in order the Dirichlet condition to be satisfied on the boundary becomes a non trivial issue. Indeed, (
2.35
) as initial data provides a non-trivial contribution on the boundary. Taking
\varkappa(-h^{2}\Delta)\varkappa(hD_{y})\delta_{(a,0)}
would be a natural choice: it may be expanded on eigenfunctions of the Laplace operator on the compact set
\Omega
, but we know very little on them. Instead, we use the spectral theory for the model Laplace operator (
2.3
) in order to expand

\varkappa(-h^{2}\Delta_{M})\varkappa(hD_{y})\delta_{(a,0)}

in terms of the eigenfunctions of
-\Delta_{M}
, as they have been used extensively in our previous work
[
9
]
,
[
12
]
. This will turn out to be sufficient for our later purposes. We now recall some properties of
-\Delta_{M}
.

2.3.1.
Spectral theory for
-\Delta_{M}
: the initial data in terms of model gallery modes

Let
-\Delta_{M}
be the Friedlander model operator introduced in (
2.3
),
and recall
q
is a positive definite quadratic form.
Taking the Fourier transform in the
y
variable, the operator
-\Delta_{M}
becomes

-\partial^{2}_{x}+|\eta|^{2}+xq(\eta)
. For
\eta\neq 0
, this operator is a positive self-adjoint operator
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, with compact resolvent. The next Lemma is proved in
[
11
]
(with
q(\eta)=|\eta|^{2}
, but only using
q(\eta)\neq 0
):

Lemma 2.17
.

(see
[
11
, Lemma 2]
)
There exists an Hilbert basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
where
\{e_{k}(x,\eta)\}_{k\geq 0}
are eigenfunctions of
-\partial^{2}_{x}+|\eta|^{2}+xq(\eta)
, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(\eta)=|\eta|^{2}+\omega_{k}q(\eta)^{2/3}=\tau_{q}^{2}(\omega_{k},\eta)
. These eigenfunctions are translated and rescaled Airy functions:

(2.82)

e_{k}(x,\eta)=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}q(\eta)^{1/6}}{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}Ai\Big(xq(\eta)^{1/3}-\omega_{k}\Big),

where
L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})
(from (
2.29
)) normalizes
\|e_{k}(.,\eta)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}=1
.

For
a>0
, the Dirac distribution
\delta_{x=a}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
may be decomposed as

\delta_{x=a}=\sum_{k\geq 1}e_{k}(x,\eta)e_{k}(a,\eta)
. Eigenfunctions of

-\partial^{2}_{x}+xq(\eta)
are
e_{k}(x,\eta)
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\eta)-|\eta|^{2}=\tau_{q}^{2}(\omega_{k},\eta)-|\eta|^{2}=\omega_{k}q^{2/3}(\eta)
, and for cut-offs
\varkappa
,
\chi_{0}
to be chosen, the following spectral decomposition holds

(2.83)

\chi_{0}(-h^{2}\partial^{2}_{x}+xq(h\partial_{y}))\varkappa(hD_{y})\delta_{(a,0)}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\int e^{iy\cdot\eta}\\
\chi_{0}\bigl(h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k}q^{\frac{2}{3}}(h\eta)\bigr)\varkappa(h\eta)e_{k}(x,\eta)e_{k}(a,\eta)d\eta\,.

With
\chi_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(-2\epsilon_{0},2\epsilon_{0})
,
\chi_{0}=1
on
[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}]
, (
2.83
) is a finite sum with
O(\epsilon_{0}/h)
terms.
Setting
\theta=h\eta
, from support considerations, taking the support of
\chi_{0}
smaller if necessary, we can assume that
\chi^{\flat}(\alpha/\epsilon_{0})\chi_{0}(\alpha q^{2/3}(\theta))\varkappa(\theta)=\chi_{0}(\alpha q^{2/3}(\theta))\varkappa(\theta)
,
where
\alpha=h^{2/3}\omega
and
\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})
is the cut-off introduced in (
2.51
) (which restricts the support of
K_{\omega}
to values
\omega\leq\epsilon_{0}/h^{2/3}
).

For
a\leq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, the easiest way to define an initial data is to chose the lefthand side term in (
2.83
), which does vanish on the boundary (
e_{k}(0,\eta)=0
for every
k\geq 1
). Using both (
2.52
) and (
2.51
), we are left to obtain a smooth function
g_{h,a}
such that for
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}
as in (
2.51
) to have

(2.84)

\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(0,x,y)=\chi_{0}(-h^{2}\partial^{2}_{x}+xq(h\partial_{y}))\varkappa(hD_{y})\delta_{(a,0)}+O_{C^{\infty}}(h^{\infty}).

We proceed as follows : for a given
K_{\epsilon}
such that
h^{-\epsilon}\ll h^{-2\epsilon}\lesssim K_{\epsilon}\lesssim h^{-1/4+\epsilon}\ll h^{-1/4}
, define
\mathcal{E}_{M}
and split the sum over
k
in (
2.83
),

\displaystyle\,\,\quad\begin{multlined}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k}):=\int e^{iy\cdot\eta}\chi_{0}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}q^{2/3}(h\eta))\varkappa(h\eta)e_{k}(x,\eta)e_{k}(a,\eta)d\eta\\
=\frac{2\pi}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int e^{iy\cdot\eta}\chi_{0}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}q^{2/3}(h\eta))\varkappa(h\eta)q^{1/3}(\eta)Ai(xq^{1/3}(\eta)-\omega_{k})Ai(aq^{1/3}(\eta)-\omega_{k})d\eta.\end{multlined}

(2.87)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}(\cdots)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi^{\flat}\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})\,.

Proposition 2.18
.

For all
a\in(0,h^{2/3-\epsilon})
there exists a smooth function
g_{h,a,1}
such that

(2.88)

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{2\pi}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega_{k}}(g_{h,a,1})(0,x,y)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi^{\flat}\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty})\,.

The proof of Proposition
2.18
is postponed to Section
2.3.3
, as it requires arguments from section
2.3.2
; we will introduce the cutoff
\chi^{\flat}({\omega_{k}}/{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}})
in the (LHS) term of (
2.88
) as well, due to how
g_{h,a,1}
is obtained.

Proposition 2.19
.

For all
a\in(0,h^{2/3-\epsilon})
, there exists a smooth function
g_{h,a,2}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\langle\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-iNL(\omega)},{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0}){\chi^{\sharp}}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})(0,x,y)\rangle_{\omega}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad{}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}/\epsilon_{0})\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty}),\end{multlined}

(2.91)

\displaystyle\langle\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-iNL(\omega)},{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0}){\chi^{\sharp}}(\omega){\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})(0,x,y)\rangle_{\omega}=O(h^{\infty}).

We introduced a new cut-off
{\chi^{\sharp}}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)
in (
2.19
) and removed
{\chi^{\sharp}}(\omega)
(which is supported for
\omega\geq 2
and identically
1
on the support of
{\chi}^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)
). The proof of Proposition
2.19
will be provided in Section
2.3.4
.
The sum of (
2.19
) and (
2.91
) yields the second term in (
2.87
). Finally, we have

Proposition 2.20
.

For all
a\in(0,h^{2/3-\epsilon})
, let
g_{h,a}:=g_{h,a,1}+g_{h,a,2}
, then
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}
, defined in Definition
2.13
is a parametrix in the sense of Definition
2.5
and (
2.84
) holds.

Proposition
2.20
follows easily from Propositions
2.18
and
2.19
using
K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,1}+g_{h,a,2})=K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,1})+K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})
together with (
2.50
).

Remark 2.21
.

For later purposes (dispersion for small
a
, Section
4
), taking
K_{\epsilon}\sim h^{-2\epsilon}
would be enough. However, in the next subsection, we aim at obtaining gallery modes for
k
as large as possible, which turns out to be up to
K_{\epsilon}\sim h^{-1/4+\epsilon}
. This is of independent interest and will prove useful to deal with the Schrödinger operator as well as generalize
[
12
]
from the model case to the general case; both will be adressed elsewhere.

2.3.2.
Pseudo-differential calculus; construction of gallery modes.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
G
be defined in (
2.14
).
We set

(2.92)

e(x,y,\eta,\omega)=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}q(\eta)^{1/6}}{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega)}}e^{-iy\cdot\eta}G(x,y,\eta,\omega).

Replacing
G(\cdots)
with
G_{M}(\cdots):=e^{iy\cdot\eta}Ai(xq(\eta)^{1/3}-\omega)
in (
2.92
) yields (
2.82
) instead of
e(x,y,\eta,\omega)
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\varkappa
be like in Definition (
2.5
). For
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
, we define

(2.93)

F_{\omega_{k}}(g)(x,y):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int e^{i(y-y^{\prime})\cdot\eta}e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})(\varkappa(hD_{y^{\prime}})g)(y^{\prime})d\eta dy^{\prime}\,.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
\tilde{\varkappa}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
be such that
\tilde{\varkappa}=1
on the support of
\varkappa
and vanishing outside a neighborhood of
\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
. Let also
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}
be a smooth cutoff supported in the ball of center
0
and radius
1/16
of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
.
For
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
we define an operator
\mathfrak{L}
as

(2.94)

\mathfrak{L}(f)(y):=\int e^{i(y-y^{\prime})\cdot\eta-i|\eta|B_{0}(y^{\prime},\eta/|\eta|)}\tilde{\varkappa}(h\eta)\chi(y^{\prime})f(y^{\prime})dy^{\prime}d\eta,

where
B_{0}
is the first term in the development of
B_{\Gamma}
in (
6.12
) and is homogeneous of degree
0
.

To define
g_{h,a,1}
and
g_{h,a,2}
, we need to ”invert”
F_{\omega_{k}}
, which requires estimating derivatives of
e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
with respect to
(y,\eta)
: in the Friedlander model case the corresponding mode
e_{k}(x,\eta)
from (
2.82
) does not depend on
y
, but here, deriving with respect to
y
yields a large factor due to
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau}B_{0}
in the phase
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau}\Gamma(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\eta)/\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau},\eta/\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau})
.
In order to get rid of the homogeneous term of degree zero
|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|/\eta|)
in the phase function of
e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
, we set
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}:=F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}
to obtain a new operator whose phase function does not include the contribution
|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|)
; we need to prove that, at least for
k\ll h^{-1/4}
, these operators can be inverted and this will be our main result in this section:

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
0<\epsilon_{1}<1/4
be small. The operators
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
are pseudo-differential operators that are uniformly elliptic with respect to
1\leq k\leq h^{-1/4+\epsilon_{1}}
.

Note that we would like to construct quasi-modes for as many
k
as possible in the next section, and therefore
\epsilon_{1}
should really be seen as very small. We momentarily postpone the proof of Proposition
2.25
to perform some preliminary steps.

We compute
F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}(f)
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
, setting
\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})=e^{-i|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|)}e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
:

\displaystyle F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}(f)(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{\eta}e^{iy\cdot\eta}e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\varkappa(h\eta)\widehat{\mathfrak{L}(f)}(\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{\eta}e^{i(y-y^{\prime})\cdot\eta+i|\eta|(B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|)-B_{0}(y^{\prime},\eta/|\eta|))}\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\varkappa(h\eta)\chi(y^{\prime})f(y^{\prime})d\eta dy^{\prime}.

Remark 2.26
.

As
y^{\prime}
is small on the support of
\chi(y^{\prime})
, there exists a cut-off
\tilde{\chi}
, supported in a ball of radius
1/8
in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, such that
\tilde{\chi}=1
on the support of
\chi
and

F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}(f)(x,y)=\tilde{\chi}(y)F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}(f)(x,y)+O(h^{\infty}).

When it will be necessary to emphasize the small size of the support in
y
of the operators we will work with, we will add the cutoff
\tilde{\chi}(y)
.

Setting
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}=F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}
, we compute the adjoint operator
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}
using that for every
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
and
\mathcal{E}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
we have
<\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f),\mathcal{E}>_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=<f,\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{E})>_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})}
. This yields

<\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f),\mathcal{E}>_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\int_{\Omega}\Big(\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{z}\int_{\eta}e^{i(y-z)\cdot\eta}e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\varkappa(h\eta)\mathfrak{L}(f)(z)d\eta dz\Big)\overline{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)dxdy\\
=\int_{y^{\prime}}f(y^{\prime})\Big(\overline{\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\eta}e^{i(y^{\prime}-y)\cdot\eta+i|\eta|(B_{0}(y^{\prime},\eta/|\eta|)-B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))}\overline{\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})}\varkappa(h\eta)\chi(y^{\prime})\mathcal{E}(x,y)d\eta dxdy}\Big)dy^{\prime}\\
=\int_{z}f(z)\overline{\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{E})}(z)dz=<f,\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{E})>_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})},

which yields
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}(\mathcal{E})
.
We can now explicitly compute
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f)(z)
for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f)(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\chi(z)}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\eta}e^{i(z-y)\cdot\eta+i|\eta|(B_{0}(z,\eta/|\eta|)-B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))}\varkappa(h\eta)\overline{\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})}\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f)(x,y)d\eta dxdy

(2.95)

\displaystyle=\int_{z^{\prime}}M_{k}(z,z^{\prime})f(z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}+O(h^{\infty}),

where we have introduced the cut-off
\tilde{\chi}(y)
and have set

M_{k}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{y,\eta,\eta^{\prime}}e^{i\big((z-y)\cdot\eta+|\eta|(B_{0}(z,\eta/|\eta|)-B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))\big)-i\big((z^{\prime}-y)\cdot\eta^{\prime}+|\eta^{\prime}|(B_{0}(z^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|)-B_{0}(y,\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|))\big)}\\
\times\tilde{\chi}(y)\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime}){\varkappa}(h\eta){\varkappa}(h\eta^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})}\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta^{\prime},\omega_{k})dxd\eta^{\prime}d\eta dy.

We let
\upsilon=\upsilon(\eta,z,y):=\eta+|\eta|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla_{y}B_{0}(\varpi y+(1-\varpi)z,\eta/|\eta|)d\varpi
(resp.
\upsilon^{\prime}=\upsilon(\eta^{\prime},z^{\prime},y)
) such that

(2.96)

\displaystyle(z-y)\upsilon=(z-y)\cdot\eta+|\eta|(B_{0}(z,\eta/|\eta|)-B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))\,,

(2.97)

\displaystyle(z^{\prime}-y)\upsilon^{\prime}=(z^{\prime}-y)\cdot\eta^{\prime}+|\eta^{\prime}|(B_{0}(z^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|)-B_{0}(y,\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|))\,.

\nabla_{\eta}\upsilon=\mathbb{I}_{d-1}+O(|\nabla_{y}B_{0}|)
being close to the identity matrix
\mathbb{I}_{d-1}
for small
y,z
,
|\nabla_{\upsilon}\eta|=1+O(|\nabla_{y}B_{0}|)
and, in the same way
|\nabla_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\eta^{\prime}|=1+O(|\nabla_{y}B_{0}|)
. Denoting
\eta(\upsilon,z,y)=\upsilon+|\upsilon|O(y,z)
the inverse function, we have
\eta=\eta(\upsilon,z,y)
and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta(\upsilon^{\prime},z^{\prime},y)
, respectively, and therefore

(2.98)

M_{k}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{y,\upsilon,\upsilon^{\prime}}e^{i(z-y)\cdot\upsilon-i(z^{\prime}-y)\cdot\upsilon^{\prime}}|\nabla_{\upsilon}\eta||\nabla_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\eta^{\prime}|\times\tilde{\chi}(y)\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime})\\
\times{\varkappa}(h\eta(\upsilon,z,y)){\varkappa}(h\eta(\upsilon^{\prime},z^{\prime},y))\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta(\upsilon,z,y),\omega_{k})}\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta(\upsilon^{\prime},z^{\prime},y),\omega_{k})dxd\upsilon^{\prime}d\upsilon dy.

Taking
y=z^{\prime}+w
,
\upsilon^{\prime}=\upsilon-\zeta
in (
2.98
), yields

(2.99)

\displaystyle M_{k}(z,z^{\prime})=\int_{\upsilon}e^{i(z-z^{\prime})\cdot\upsilon}m_{k}(z,z^{\prime},\upsilon)d\upsilon\,,

\displaystyle m_{k}(z,z^{\prime},\upsilon)=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int_{\zeta}\int_{w}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-iw\cdot\zeta}|\nabla_{\upsilon}\eta|(\upsilon,z,z^{\prime}+w)|\nabla_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\eta|(\upsilon-\zeta,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)\\
\times{\varkappa}(h\eta(\upsilon,z,z^{\prime}+w)){\varkappa}(h\eta^{\prime}(\upsilon-\zeta,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w))\tilde{\chi}(z^{\prime}+w)\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime})\\
\times\overline{\tilde{e}(x,z^{\prime}+w,\eta(\upsilon,z,z^{\prime}+w),\omega_{k})}\tilde{e}(x,z^{\prime}+w,\eta(\upsilon-\zeta,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w),\omega_{k})dxdwd\zeta.\end{multlined}

On the support of the two cut-offs
\varkappa
,
h(\upsilon+O(z,z^{\prime}+w))\in[1/2,2]
,
h(\upsilon-\zeta+O(z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w))\in[1/2,2]
, and on the support of
\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime}+w)\tilde{\chi}(z^{\prime}+w)
,
|z,z^{\prime}|<1/16,|z^{\prime}+w|<1/8
; then, set
\upsilon=\frac{\theta}{h}
,
\zeta=\frac{\varrho}{h}
where
\theta\in[1/4,5/4]
and
|\varrho|\leq 2
. In (
2.99
) we may replace
m_{k}(z,z^{\prime},\upsilon)
by
m_{k}(z^{\prime},z^{\prime},\upsilon)=:\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\upsilon)
without changing the integral modulo
O(\upsilon^{-\infty})=O(h^{\infty})
. In the new variables (and modulo
O(h^{\infty})
terms), the symbol of (
2.99
) becomes

(2.103)

\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta/h)=\frac{1}{(2\pi h)^{d-1}}\int_{\varrho}\int_{w}e^{-\frac{i}{h}w\cdot\varrho}a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(w,\varrho);h)dwd\varrho\,,

where

(2.104)

a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(w,\varrho);h):=|\nabla_{\upsilon}\eta|(\theta/h,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)|\nabla_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\eta|((\theta-\varrho)/h,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)\\
\times{\varkappa}(h\eta(\theta/h,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)){\varkappa}(h\eta((\theta-\varrho)/h,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}-w))\tilde{\chi}(z^{\prime}+w)\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime})\\
\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{\tilde{e}(x,z^{\prime}+w,\eta(\theta/h,z,z^{\prime}+w),\omega_{k})}\tilde{e}(x,z^{\prime}+w,\eta((\theta-\varrho)/h,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w),\omega_{k})dx\,.

Define

(2.105)

\mathcal{S}_{\gamma}:=\{a\in C^{\infty}\,\,\text{such that}\,\,|\partial_{w}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{\varrho}^{\beta_{2}}a(w,\varrho,h)|\leq C_{\beta}h^{-\gamma(|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|)}\}\,.

We now prove

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
0<\epsilon_{1}<1/4
be small. The symbols
a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(w,\varrho);h)
are in the class
\mathcal{S}_{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{3}\epsilon_{1}}
, uniformly with respect to
1\leq k\leq h^{-\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon_{1}}
.

Proof.

We check that there exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that for every
|\beta|\geq 1
,

(2.106)

h^{|\beta|}\Big|\partial^{\beta}_{w}\partial^{\beta}_{\varrho}a_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta;w,\varrho;h)|_{w=0,\varrho=0}\Big|\lesssim h^{\frac{4}{3}\epsilon_{1}|\beta|}\,.

This easily follows from following lemma whose proof is postponed to the Appendix:

Lemma 2.28
.

Uniformly for
h^{2/3}\omega_{k}\ll 1
, we have:

(2.107)

\|\partial^{\beta}_{(y,\theta)}\tilde{e}(.,y,\theta/h,\omega_{k})\|_{L^{2}(x\geq 0)}\lesssim\big(\omega_{k}/h^{1/3}\big)^{|\beta|}\,.

Using (
2.104
), (
2.107
), and
\omega_{k}\sim k^{2/3}
, we get that for every
|\beta|\geq 1
, as
k\leq h^{-1/4+\epsilon_{1}}
.

h^{|\beta|}\Big|\partial^{\beta}_{w}\partial^{\beta}_{\varrho}a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(w,\varrho);h)|_{w=0,\varrho=0}\Big|\lesssim C_{\beta}h^{|\beta|}\big(\omega_{k}/h^{1/3}\big)^{2|\beta|}\lesssim C_{\beta}\big(h^{4\epsilon_{1}/3}\big)^{|\beta|}\,,

which proves the Lemma and completes the proof of Proposition
2.27
.
∎

We now turn our attention to
\tilde{m}_{k}
and recall the classical expansion:

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
a(w,\varrho,h):\mathbb{R}^{2n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be
C^{\infty}
with
a\in\mathcal{S}_{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon_{1}}
, with small
\epsilon_{1}>0
. Then

\frac{1}{(2\pi h)^{n}}\int_{\varrho}\int_{w}e^{-\frac{i}{h}w\cdot\varrho}a(w,\varrho,h)dwd\varrho=\sum_{\beta}\frac{h^{|\beta|}}{i^{|\beta|}|\beta|!}\partial^{\beta}_{w}\partial_{\varrho}^{\beta}a(w,\varrho,h)\Big|_{(w,\varrho)=(0,0)}\,.

Using Lemma
2.29
for
n=d-1
with
a_{k}
defined in (
2.104
) and Proposition
2.27
, we get that
\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta/h)
may be written as

(2.108)

\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta/h)=\sum_{\beta}\frac{h^{|\beta|}}{i^{|\beta|}|\beta|!}\partial^{\beta}_{w}\partial^{\beta}_{\varrho}a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(w,\varrho);h)|_{w=0,\varrho=0}\,.

We may now return to the proof of our main Proposition.

Proof.

(of Proposition
2.25
)
From (
2.3.2
) and (
2.99
),

(2.109)

\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f)(z)=\int e^{\frac{i}{h}(z-z^{\prime})\cdot\theta}\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta/h)f(z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}\,,

where the symbol
\tilde{m}_{k}(z^{\prime},\theta/h)
is given by (
2.108
) and where, for every
k\leq h^{-1/4-\epsilon_{1}}
, (
2.106
) holds true. Moreover,
\tilde{m}_{k}
is elliptic. Indeed, from (
2.104
), it follows that

a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(0,0);h)={\sigma}(z^{\prime},\theta/h;0,0)\|\tilde{e}(.,z^{\prime},\theta/h,\omega_{k})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(x>0)},

where for
\upsilon=\theta/h
and
\zeta=\varrho/h
like before, we define

(2.110)

\sigma((z^{\prime},\upsilon);(w,\zeta))=|\nabla_{\upsilon}\eta|(\upsilon,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)|\nabla_{\upsilon^{\prime}}\eta|(\upsilon-\zeta,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)\\
\times{\varkappa}(h\eta(\upsilon,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}+w)){\varkappa}(h\eta(\upsilon-\zeta,z^{\prime},z^{\prime}-w))\tilde{\chi}(z^{\prime}+w)\chi(z)\chi(z^{\prime}).

Using Lemma
6.1
in the Appendix,
the
e(.,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
are almost
L^{2}
-normalized in
x>0
and
\|\tilde{e}(\cdot,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\|_{L^{2}(x>0)}\sim 1
. On the other hand the symbol
\sigma
defined in (
2.110
) is elliptic, therefore
a_{k}
is elliptic and
a_{k}((z^{\prime},\theta);(0,0);h)\sim 1
for
\theta
close to
1
; using (
2.106
),
\tilde{m}_{k}
is therefore elliptic;
\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}^{*}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}
are pseudo-differential operators, uniformly elliptic for
k\leq h^{-1/4+\epsilon_{1}}
.
∎

2.3.3.
Construction of quasi-modes
k\ll h^{-1/4}
(proof of Proposition
2.18
).

It suffices to construct a smooth function
g_{h,a,1}
such that, for all
k
such that
\chi_{\flat}(\omega_{k}/\omega_{K_{\epsilon}})\neq 0
,

(2.111)

\frac{2\pi}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}K_{\omega_{k}}(g_{h,a,1})(0,x,y)=\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty}).

Indeed, for a function
g(y^{\prime},\varrho)
, let
\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}(y^{\prime},\alpha/h)
be its Fourier transform w.r.t.
\varrho
at
\alpha/h
.
Using the definition of
K_{\omega}(g)
, we only need
\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(.,\omega/h^{1/3})
for
\omega\in\{\omega_{k},1\leq k\ll h^{-1/4}\}
.

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
h^{-2\epsilon}\lesssim K_{\epsilon}\lesssim h^{-1/4+\epsilon}
,
\epsilon>0
small. For
1\leq k\leq 4K_{\epsilon}
, define

(2.112)

f_{\omega_{k}}(y):=\Big(\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}\Big)^{-1}\Big(\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}(\mathcal{E}_{M}(.,\omega_{k}))\Big)(y)\,,

then define
g_{h,a,1}
with:
\forall k
such that
\chi^{\flat}(\omega_{k}/\omega_{K_{\epsilon}})\neq 0
,

(2.113)

\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(.,\omega_{k}/h^{1/3}):=\frac{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathfrak{L}(f_{\omega_{k}})\,,\\

and
\forall k
such that
\chi^{\flat}(\omega_{k}/\omega_{K_{\epsilon}})=0
,

\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(.,\omega_{k}/h^{1/3}):=0
. Then Proposition
2.18

holds for
g_{h,a,1}
.

Proof.

As
k\leq 4K_{\epsilon}\lesssim h^{-1/4+\epsilon}\ll h^{-1/4}
, we can use Proposition
2.25
and define
f_{\omega_{k}}
by (
2.112
). For such
f_{\omega_{k}}
and
\chi_{\flat}(\omega_{k}/\omega_{K_{\epsilon}})\neq 0
, we now define
\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(y,\omega_{k}/h^{1/3})
by (
2.113
) (and zero for larger
k
’s).

By construction, on the support of
\chi_{\flat}(\omega_{k}/\omega_{K_{\epsilon}})
, we have
F_{\omega_{k}}(\mathfrak{L}(f_{\omega_{k}}))(x,y)=\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f_{\omega_{k}})(x,y)=\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})
, as we chose
f_{\omega_{k}}
such that
\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f_{\omega_{k}})=\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}(\mathcal{E}_{M}(.,\omega_{k}))
. In turn, we have

(2.114)

F_{\omega_{k}}\Big(\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(.,\omega_{k}/h^{1/3})\Big)(x,y)=\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty})\,,

(inverting
\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}
); using (
2.31
) and (
2.93
),
\frac{2\pi}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}K_{\omega_{k}}(g_{h,a,1})(0,x,y)=\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty})
.
∎

We obtain the explicit form of
\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}
as a corollary of Lemma
2.30
:

Corollary 2.31
.

We keep the notations from the proof of Lemma
2.30
.
Let

(2.115)

I_{a}(\eta,\omega_{k}):=\int_{x,y}e^{-iy\cdot\eta}\overline{e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})dxdy.

For
1\leq k\leq K_{\epsilon}
,
g_{h,a,1}
(from Lemma
2.30
) may be rewritten

\overset{\lower 7.37497pt\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!\!2\wedge}$}}{g}_{h,a,1}(y^{\prime},\omega_{k}/h^{1/3})=\frac{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int e^{iy^{\prime}\cdot\eta}\varkappa(h\eta)r(\eta,\omega_{k})I_{a}(\eta,\omega_{k})d\eta,

where
r(.,\omega_{k})
is an elliptic symbol of order
0
and main contribution
1/\tilde{m}_{k}(y,\eta+|\eta|\partial_{y}B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))
with
\tilde{m}_{k}
defined in (
2.108
).

Proof.

We compute explicitly

\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}(\mathcal{E}_{M})(z)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int e^{i(z\cdot\eta+|\eta|B_{0}(z,\eta/|\eta|))}\varkappa(h\eta)\int_{x,y}e^{-iy\cdot\eta}\overline{e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})dxdyd\eta.

Moreover, using (
2.3.2
) and
2.99
, there exists an elliptic symbol
\tilde{r}_{k}(y^{\prime},\upsilon)
of order
0
with main contribution
1/\tilde{m}_{k}
and
\tilde{\varkappa}
supported for
\upsilon\sim\frac{1}{h}
and equal to
1
on the support of
\varkappa
such that
(\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}\circ\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}})^{-1}(F)(y^{\prime})=\int e^{i(y^{\prime}-z)\cdot\eta}\tilde{\varkappa}(h\eta)\tilde{r}_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)F(z)dzd\eta
. Taking
F=\tilde{F}^{*}_{\omega_{k}}(\mathcal{E}_{M})
yields

f_{\omega_{k}}(y^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int e^{i(y^{\prime}-z)\cdot\eta}\tilde{r}_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)\varkappa(h\eta^{\prime})\int e^{i(z\cdot\eta^{\prime}+|\eta^{\prime}|B_{0}(z,\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|))}I_{a}(\eta^{\prime},\omega_{k})d\eta^{\prime}dzd\eta.

Applying stationary phase with respect to
z,\eta
, critical points are
z=y^{\prime}
,
\eta=\eta^{\prime}+|\eta^{\prime}|\partial_{z}B_{0}(z,\eta^{\prime}/|\eta^{\prime}|)|_{z=y^{\prime}}
and

f_{\omega_{k}}(y^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int e^{i(y^{\prime}\cdot\eta+|\eta|B_{0}(y^{\prime},\eta/|\eta|))}r_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)I_{a}(\eta,\omega_{k})d\eta

where the new symbol
r_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)
is obtained from
\tilde{r}_{k}
and has main contribution
\tilde{r}_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)=1/\tilde{m}_{k}
and where
I
remains unchanged since depended only on
\eta^{\prime}
and not on
x,\eta
. We therefore get

\mathfrak{L}(f_{\omega_{k}})(y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\int e^{i(y-y^{\prime})\cdot\tilde{\eta}-|\tilde{\eta}|B_{0}(y^{\prime},\tilde{\eta}/|\tilde{\eta}|)}\varkappa(h\tilde{\eta})\int e^{i(y^{\prime}\cdot\eta+|\eta|B_{0}(y^{\prime},\eta/|\eta|))}r_{k}(y^{\prime},\eta)I_{a}(\eta,\omega_{k})d\eta dy^{\prime}d\tilde{\eta}

and integrating in
y^{\prime},\tilde{\eta}
give
y^{\prime}=y
,
\tilde{\eta}=\eta
achieves the proof.
∎

2.3.4.
Proof of Proposition
2.19

Our goal is to obtain
g_{h,a,2}
such that (
2.19
) holds. Observe that the sum in the second line of (
2.19
), involving
\mathcal{E}_{M}
for large
\omega_{k}
, is, using (
2.30
),

(2.116)

\sum_{k\geq 1}\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega_{k}}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k}/\epsilon_{0})\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega_{k})\\
=\langle\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-iNL(\omega)},{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\frac{L^{\prime}(\omega)}{2\pi}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega))\rangle_{\omega},

and non-stationary phase (with respect to
\omega
) easily applies for all
|N|\geq 2
in the second line of (
2.116
), providing an
O(h^{\infty})
contribution (this is just the model case). Therefore, we are left to obtain
g_{h,a,2}
such that

(2.117)

\int{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega)\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(1+\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm iL(\omega)}\Big)K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})(0,x,y)d\omega\\
+\sum_{|N|\geq 2}\int e^{-iNL(\omega)}{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{\epsilon}\omega)K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})(0,x,y)d\omega\\
=\int{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\Big(1+\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm iL(\omega)}\Big)\frac{L^{\prime}(\omega)}{2\pi}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega)d\omega+O(h^{\infty}).

Let us analyze the last line of (
2.117
), corresponding to the sum over model gallery modes. Here
\mathcal{E}_{M}
is a product of two Airy functions
e^{iy\cdot\eta}\mathrm{Ai}(-\zeta_{M}(x,\eta,\omega))\mathrm{Ai}(-\zeta_{M}(a,\eta,\omega))
, where the phases
\zeta_{M}=\omega-xq^{1/3}(\eta)
and
\psi_{M}(y,\eta)=y\cdot\eta
are such that (
2.7
) holds with
<.,.>
replaced by the scalar product obtained by polarization of the principal symbol
\xi^{2}+|\eta|^{2}+xq(\eta)
of the model Laplace operator
\Delta_{M}
. Using the definition of
L
in Lemma
2.9
,

1+\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm iL(\omega)}=1-\Big(\frac{A_{+}}{A_{-}}\Big)(\omega)-\Big(\frac{A_{-}}{A_{+}}\Big)(\omega)
. As
h^{2/3}\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}\sim(hK_{\epsilon})^{2/3}\gtrsim(h^{1-2\epsilon})^{2/3}\gg h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, it follows that
h^{2/3}\omega
is much larger than
a
on the support of the symbol of the integral in the last line of (
2.117
), and we can use (
2.27
) to write
Ai(-\zeta_{M}(a,\eta,\omega))=\sum_{\pm}A_{\pm}(\zeta_{M}(a,\eta,\omega))
. The phase of
\mathcal{E}_{M}
is now

(2.118)

y\cdot\eta+\xi^{3}/3+\xi(xq^{1/3}(\eta)-\omega)\pm\frac{2}{3}(\omega-aq^{1/3}(\eta))^{3/2}.

Lemma 2.32
.

In the integral defining
\mathcal{E}_{M}(.,\omega)
, the usual stationary phase in
\xi
applies. Moreover, for the phase corresponding to
N=0
in the second line of (
2.116
), we have

\phi_{M,\pm,\mp}(x,y,\eta,\omega):=y\cdot\eta\pm\frac{2}{3}\Big((\omega-xq^{1/3}(\eta))^{3/2}-(\omega-aq^{1/3}(\eta))^{3/2}\Big).

In the same way, the phases corresponding to
N=\pm 1
in the second line of (
2.116
) are

\phi_{M,\pm,\pm}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\mp\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}:=y\cdot\eta\pm\frac{2}{3}\Big((\omega-xq^{1/3}(\eta))^{3/2}+(\omega-aq^{1/3}(\eta))^{3/2}-2\omega^{3/2}\Big).

Moreover, for
x>2h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, the integral in the second line of (
2.117
) is
O(h^{\infty})
.

Proof.

Let
N=0
. The derivative with respect to
\omega
of the phase (
2.118
) of
\mathcal{E}_{M}
vanishes when
\xi=\pm\sqrt{\omega-aq^{1/3}(\eta)}
. As
a\leq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
and
\omega\geq\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}\gtrsim h^{-2\epsilon}\gg a/h^{2/3}
, we introduce a cut-off in
\xi
localizing for
|\xi|\in[\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\omega},2\sqrt{\omega}]
without changing the contribution of the integral corresponding to
N=0
modulo
O(h^{\infty})
(for
|\xi|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\omega}
the phase (
2.118
) is non-stationary in
\omega
). The second derivative of (
2.118
) with respect to
\xi
is
2\xi\sim\pm\sqrt{\omega}
and for
\xi
such that
|\xi|\sim\sqrt{\omega}\geq\sqrt{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\gg h^{-\epsilon}
on the support of the symbol, stationary phase yields the following phase for
N=0
,

\phi_{M,\pm,\pm}(x,y,\eta,\omega):=y\cdot\eta\pm\frac{2}{3}(\omega-xq(\eta)^{1/3})^{3/2}\pm\frac{2}{3}(\omega-aq(\eta)^{1/3})^{3/2}\,.

We further notice that the phases
\phi_{M,+,+}
and
\phi_{M,-,-}
are non-stationary in
\omega
: indeed,
\partial_{\omega}\phi_{M,+,+}\sim 2\sqrt{\omega}
and we get
O(h^{\infty})
by integrations by parts. Moreover, taking switching signs, the derivative with respect to
\omega
becomes

(2.119)

\sqrt{\omega-xq(\eta)^{1/3}}-\sqrt{\omega-aq(\eta)^{1/3}}\sim\frac{(a-x)}{2\sqrt{\omega}}q(\eta)^{1/3}\,,

and from (
2.119
), for
x-a\geq 2h^{2/3-\epsilon}-a\geq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
and
\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}\lesssim\omega\leq\epsilon_{0}h^{-2/3}
, integrations by parts in
\omega
provide a
O(h^{\infty})
contribution in the integral corresponding to
N=0
in the second line of (
2.116
). In fact, with
\mathcal{E}_{M}
as in (
2.3.1
), the symbol of the integral in the second line of (
2.116
) depends on
\omega
only through
\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}({\omega}/{\omega_{k}})
, and therefore, in order to integrate by parts in
\omega
, it remains to check that, for some
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
, we have

(2.120)

\frac{2\sqrt{\omega}}{(x-a)q(\eta)^{1/3}}\partial_{\omega}\Big(\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\Big)\ll h^{\tilde{\epsilon}}\,.

As
\chi^{\sharp}({\omega}/{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}})
is constant everywhere but for
\omega\sim\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}
, (
2.120
) vanishes everywhere but for
\omega\sim\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}
; from
\eta\sim\frac{1}{h}
and
\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}\sim K_{\epsilon}^{2/3}\gg h^{-4\epsilon/3}
, the lefthand side in (
2.120
) is at most
\frac{h^{2/3}}{h^{2/3-\epsilon}\sqrt{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}}
;
we obtain (
2.120
) with
\tilde{\epsilon}=5\epsilon/3
and we can integrate by parts infinitely many times.

For
N=\pm 1
we proceed in a similar manner. Let for instance
N=1
, then the phase of
e^{-iL(\omega)}\mathcal{E}_{M}
is just (
2.118
)
-\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}
; it is stationary with respect to
\omega
for
\xi=2\sqrt{\omega}-\sqrt{\omega-aq^{1/3}(\eta)}
. As
\omega\geq\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}\gtrsim h^{-4\epsilon/3}\gg a/h^{2/3}
we again introduce a cut-off, supported for
|\xi|\in[\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\omega},2\sqrt{\omega}]
without changing the contribution of the integral modulo
O(h^{\infty})
. Stationary phase then applies in
\xi
and provides the phase function
\phi_{M,\pm,\pm}-\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}
. We easily see that
\phi_{M,\pm,\mp}
and
\phi_{M,-,-}
are non-stationary with respect to
\omega
and provide a
O(h^{\infty})
contribution. We are left with
\phi_{M,+,+}-\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}
whose derivative with respect to
\omega
is

(2.121)

\sqrt{\omega-xq(\eta)^{1/3}}+\sqrt{\omega-aq(\eta)^{1/3}}-2\sqrt{\omega}\sim-(x+a)q(\eta)^{1/3}/(2\sqrt{\omega})\,.

From
a\lesssim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
we obtain that, for
x\geq 2h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, the phase
\phi_{M,+,+}-\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}
is non-stationary in
\omega
and yields an
O(h^{\infty})
contribution. The exact same line of reasoning applies to
N=-1
.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

For
|x,a|\leq\frac{h^{2/3}}{\sqrt{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}}
, we cannot get an
O(h^{\infty})
contribution for
N=\pm 1
: (
2.120
) does not hold anymore even though for
x\geq 0
the derivative with respect to
\omega
does not vanish; for such small values of
a
we cannot perform integrations by parts. Specifically, as
K_{\epsilon}\ll h^{-1/4}
, we are to deal with this case for all
|x,a|\leq h^{2/3+1/12}=h^{3/4}(\ll\frac{h^{2/3}}{\sqrt{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}})
.

Putting all this together, the integral in the last line in (
2.117
) reads as

\int{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\varepsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}\Big(\frac{\omega}{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big)\Big(1+\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm iL(\omega)}\Big)\frac{L^{\prime}(\omega)}{2\pi}\mathcal{E}_{M}(x,y,a,\omega)d\omega=E_{M,+}(x,y,a)+E_{M,-}(x,y,a),

where we have set, modulo
O(h^{\infty})
,

(2.122)

E_{M,+}(x,y,a):=\int{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\varepsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}({\omega}/{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}})\int q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\chi_{0}(h^{2/3}\omega q^{2/3}(h\eta))\\
\times e^{iy\cdot\eta}\Big(A_{+}(\zeta_{M}(x,\eta,\omega))-\Big(\frac{A_{+}}{A_{-}}\Big)(\omega)A_{-}(\zeta_{M}(x,\eta,\omega))\Big)A_{-}(\zeta_{M}(a,\eta,\omega))d\eta d\omega,

(2.123)

E_{M,-}(x,y,a):=\int{\chi}^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\varepsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}({\omega}/{\omega_{K_{\epsilon}}})\int q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\chi_{0}(h^{2/3}\omega q^{2/3}(h\eta))\\
\times e^{iy\cdot\eta}\Big(A_{-}(\zeta_{M}(x,\eta,\omega))-\Big(\frac{A_{-}}{A_{+}}\Big)(\omega)A_{+}(\zeta_{M}(x,\eta,\omega))\Big)A_{+}(\zeta_{M}(a,\eta,\omega))d\eta d\omega,

where the phase functions of the Airy terms in the second line of (
2.122
) are
\phi_{M,+,-}
and
\phi_{M,-,-}+L(\omega)
, while the phase functions of the Airy terms in the second line of (
2.123
) are
\phi_{M,-,+}
and
\phi_{M,+,+}-L(\omega)
. Moreover, for
x>2h^{2/3-\epsilon}
,
E_{M,\pm}(x,y,a)=O(h^{\infty})
and
E_{M,\pm}(0,y,a)=0
. This means that we can introduce a smooth cut-off
\chi_{1}(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon})
with
\chi_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{0}
equal to
1
on
[-1,1]
and equal to
0
for
x\geq 2h^{2/3-\epsilon}
such that
E_{M,\pm}(x,y,a)=\chi_{1}(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon})E_{M,\pm}(x,y,a)+O(h^{\infty})
, and therefore we need to construct
g_{h,a,2}
such that (
2.117
) holds with the last line replaced by
\chi_{1}(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon})(E_{M,+}(x,y,a)+E_{M,-}(x,y,a))
(instead of
E_{M,+}(x,y,a)+E_{M,-}(x,y,a)
).

We now go back to (
2.117
): the symbol of its left hand side has support in
\omega\gtrsim h^{-2\epsilon}
, while the right hand side is essentially supported for
x,a\lesssim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
. For such values of
x
and
\omega
we have
\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)=\omega-x|\eta|^{2/3}e_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})\geq\omega/2
; using (
2.8
) we write

G(x,y,\eta,\omega)=e^{i\psi}\sum_{\pm}\Big(p_{0}A_{\pm}(\zeta)+i|\eta|^{-1/3}p_{1}A^{\prime}_{\pm}(\zeta)\Big)=:G_{\pm}(x,y,\eta,\omega).

Proposition 2.34
.

There exists smooth functions
g_{h,a,2,\pm}
such that, with
g_{h,a,2}:=\sum_{\pm}g_{h,a,2,\pm}
,

(2.124)

\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(G_{+}(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\Big(\frac{A_{+}}{A_{-}}\Big)(\omega)G_{-}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\Big)\\
q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\hat{g}_{h,a,2,-}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3})d\eta d\omega=E_{M,+}(x,y,a)+O(h^{\infty}),

(2.125)

\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(G_{-}(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\Big(\frac{A_{-}}{A_{+}}\Big)(\omega)G_{+}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\Big)\\
\times q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\hat{g}_{h,a,2,+}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3})d\eta d\omega=E_{M,-}(x,y,a)+O(h^{\infty}).

(2.126)

\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(G_{+}(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\Big(\frac{A_{+}}{A_{-}}\Big)(\omega)G_{-}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\Big)\\
q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\hat{g}_{h,a,2,+}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3})d\eta d\omega=O(h^{\infty}),

(2.127)

\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(G_{-}(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\Big(\frac{A_{-}}{A_{+}}\Big)(\omega)G_{+}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\Big)\\
\times q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\hat{g}_{h,a,2,-}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3})d\eta d\omega=O(h^{\infty}),

(2.128)

\sum_{|N|\geq 2}\int e^{-iNL(\omega)}\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)K_{\omega}(g_{h,a,2})(0,x,y)d\omega=O(h^{\infty}).

Proof.

Proving that both operators in the first lines of (
2.125
) and (
2.124
) are invertible is sufficient: once we define
g_{h,a,2,\pm}
it will be clear that (
2.126
), (
2.127
) and (
2.128
) hold (using non-stationary phase arguments). In fact, we notice from (
2.124
) that
g_{h,a,2,-}
has to have a phase function whose derivative with respect to
\omega
should equal
2\sqrt{\omega}+O(a)
, since otherwise the phase of (
2.124
) is non-stationary in
\omega
. Introducing such a function
\hat{g}_{h,a,2,-}
in the integral in the first line of (
2.127
) yields a phase function (for (
2.127
)) whose derivative with respect to
\omega
behaves like
4\sqrt{\omega}
and since
\omega
is large on the support of the symbol this allows to perform repeated integrations by part with respect to
\omega
to obtain a
O(h^{\infty})
contribution. In the same way we prove (
2.128
), since for
|N|\geq 2
all the phase functions will be non-stationary in
\omega
and after each integration by parts we obtain a factor
(N\sqrt{\omega})^{-1}
, which will allow to sum up over
N
to conclude. We are reduced to proving that we can define
g_{h,a,2,-}
satisfying (
2.124
) (solving (
2.125
) follows in exactly the same way).

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
\tilde{J}_{+}:=J_{+}+R_{+}
, with

(2.129)

J_{+}(f)(x,y)=\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp})(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)G_{+}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi_{1}(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon})\\
\times q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))e^{i(y^{\prime}\cdot\eta+\varrho\omega/h^{1/3})}f(y^{\prime},\varrho)d\eta d\omega dy^{\prime}d\varrho.

(2.130)

R_{+}(f)(x,y)=\int\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})\chi^{\sharp})(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\Big(\frac{A_{+}}{A_{-}}\Big)(\omega)G_{-}(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi_{1}(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon})\\
\times q(\eta)^{1/6}\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))e^{i(y^{\prime}\cdot\eta+\varrho\omega/h^{1/3})}f(y^{\prime},\varrho)d\eta d\omega dy^{\prime}d\varrho.

The operator
\tilde{J}_{+}
is well defined from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{y^{\prime},\varrho}
into the space of functions of
(x,y)
near
(0,0)
, and with
h
as small parameter,
J_{+}
is an elliptic semi-classical Fourier integral operator. Moreover,
\|J_{+}^{-1}\circ R_{+}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2})}=O(h^{\infty})
, hence
\tilde{J}_{+}
is invertible and
\tilde{J}^{-1}_{+}=\Big(I+J^{-1}_{+}\circ R_{+}\Big)^{-1}\circ J^{-1}_{+}
.

If we now chose
g_{h,a,2,-}(y^{\prime},\varrho):=\tilde{J}_{+}^{-1}(E_{M,+})
, this achieves the proof of Proposition
2.34
.
∎

Proof.

(of Proposition
2.35
)
The operator
J_{+}
is easily elliptic and invertible with phase function
\psi+\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}
with
\psi
and
\zeta
defined in Theorem
2.1
. The phase function of
R_{+}
is
\psi-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}+\frac{4}{3}\omega^{3/2}
. Therefore the phase function of
J^{-1}_{+}\circ R_{+}
is given by

(2.131)

-\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}(x,y,\eta,\omega)+\psi(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})-\frac{2}{3}\zeta^{3/2}(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})+\frac{4}{3}\tilde{\omega}^{3/2},

where
x,y
are now integration variables. The derivative of (
2.131
) with respect to
x
is

-\partial_{x}\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)\sqrt{\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)}-\partial_{x}\zeta(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})\sqrt{\zeta(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})}-\partial_{x}\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega)+\partial_{x}\psi(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega}),

where
|\eta|,|\tilde{\eta}|\sim 1/h
,
\omega,\tilde{\omega}\geq h^{-2\epsilon}
and
xq^{1/3}(\eta)\leq h^{2/3-\epsilon-2/3}=h^{-\epsilon}
on the support of the symbol. As
\zeta=\omega-x|\eta|^{2/3}e_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega/|\eta|^{2/3})
with
e_{0}
elliptic and close to
1
, the derivatives of the two terms involving
\zeta
in (
2.131
) are such that

\Big|\partial_{x}\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)\sqrt{\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)}+\partial_{x}\zeta(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})\sqrt{\zeta(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})}\Big|\gtrsim\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{\omega}|\eta|^{2/3}+\sqrt{\tilde{\omega}}|\tilde{\eta}|^{2/3}).

On the other hand, using Corollary
6.10
and (
6.49
) in particular, the derivative with respect to
x
of
\psi
is of the form

\partial_{x}\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega)=\mu(y,\eta/|\eta|)q^{2/3}(\eta)/\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)\Big(\omega+2xq^{1/3}(1+\ell(y,\eta/|\eta|))+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4}\Big),

and therefore

\Big|\partial_{x}\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega)-\partial_{x}\psi(x,y,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})\Big|\lesssim\omega|\eta|^{1/3}+\tilde{\omega}|\tilde{\eta}|^{1/3},

where we have used that
q^{2/3}(\eta)/\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)=|\eta|^{4/3}q^{2/3}(\eta/|\eta|)/(|\eta|\tau_{q}(\omega/|\eta|^{2/3},\eta/|\eta|))\sim|\eta|^{1/3}
. On the support of the symbol
\chi(h^{2/3}\omega)\chi(h^{2/3}\tilde{\omega})
we have
|\omega|,|\tilde{\omega}|\leq\epsilon_{0}h^{-2/3}
, which means that the main contribution of the derivative of (
2.131
) comes from the terms involving
\zeta
and behaves like
\simeq(\sqrt{\omega}|\eta|^{2/3}+\sqrt{\tilde{\omega}}|\tilde{\eta}|^{2/3})
, as for
|\eta|,|\tilde{\eta}|\simeq 1/h
we have
\sqrt{\omega}|\eta|^{2/3}\gg\omega|\eta|^{1/3}
(
\omega h^{2/3}\ll 1
). To perform non stationary phase and obtain an
O(h^{\infty})
contribution, we check that taking one derivative with respect to
x
of the symbol provides a factor
O(h^{\epsilon})
. Indeed,
{h^{2/3}}\partial_{x}(\chi(x/h^{2/3-\epsilon}))/{\sqrt{\omega}}\sim h^{\epsilon}/\sqrt{\omega}\leq h^{\frac{5}{3}\epsilon}
, which completes the proof.
∎

To complete the proof of Proposition
2.19
, it remains to prove that, for
g_{h,a,2,-}:=\tilde{J}^{-1}_{+}(E_{M,+})
, (
2.91
) holds: but then in (
2.91
) one obtains a vanishing symbol as
\chi^{\flat}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)\chi^{\sharp}(h^{2\epsilon}\omega)=0
.∎

3.
Dispersion estimates when
a\geq h^{2/3-\epsilon}

Here again one should think of
\epsilon
as being very small: we may set
0<\epsilon<1/12
to be consistent with the parametrix construction we just did in the opposite regime (subsection
2.3
), to have an overlap between both regimes where we get dispersion estimates by different arguments. We now use the parametrix as a sum over
N
to obtain the following dispersion estimates, restricting to positive times for the sake of simplicity.

Theorem 3.1
.

There exist
a_{0}
,
c
,
C
,
\epsilon
such that for all
|(t,x,y,h,a)|<a_{0}
, one has

•

for
t\leq c\sqrt{a}
,

(3.1)

|\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)|\leq Ch^{-d}\min\left(1,(h/t)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\right)\,;

•

For
a\geq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
,
t>c\sqrt{a}
,

(3.2)

|\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)|\leq Ch^{-d}\left(\frac{h}{t}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\left((\max(a,x))^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{h}{t}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}+h^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\,;

•

For
a\leq h^{1/3+\epsilon}
,

(3.3)

|\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)|\leq Ch^{-d}\left(\frac{h}{t}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2}+\frac{1}{3}}\,.

The first estimate, (
3.1
), is just the (short time) dispersion for a free wave. On this timescale, the wave has at most one reflection and singularities have not appeared yet. One should point out that (a suitable version of) such dispersion is already proved in
[
2
]
, for a more general boundary.

The second estimate, (
3.2
), is proved using the parametrix as a sum over reflected waves. The first term is due to swallowtail singularities (and always larger than the corresponding factor in the free dispersion) and the second term is due to the presence of cusps appearing after each swallowtail singularity, between two consecutive reflections; notice that here we use the parametrix construction in an extended region
a\geq h^{2/3-\epsilon}
(when compared to the previous parametrix construction in
[
9
]
, where it was obtained as a superposition of waves only for
a\geq h^{4/7-\epsilon}
).

The third estimate, (
3.3
), will be proved using the parametrix as a sum over quasi-modes, and we postpone its proof to the last section, where we deal with decay of such quasi-modes.

We start with a lemma which allows to deal with the parametrix “behind the wave front”.

Lemma 3.2
.

There exist
c_{0}
and
T_{0}
such that , with
\mathcal{B}=\{0\leq x\leq a,|y|\leq c_{0}t,0<h\leq t\}
,

(3.4)

\forall t\in[0,T_{0}]\,,\quad\quad\sup_{x,y,t\in\mathcal{B}}|\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)|\leq Ch^{-d}O((h/t)^{\infty})\,.

Proof.

The lemma follows from classical propagation of singularities: for
t\leq a/C_{1}
with a large
C_{1}
, we apply Melrose-Sjöstrand’s theorem in the interior of
\Omega
.
Now, let
T_{0}
be small enough and consider a given
s\in[h,T_{0}]
. Rescale with
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
as new variables and
\hbar
a new parameter:
t=st^{\prime}
,
x=sx^{\prime}
,
y=sy^{\prime}
,
\hbar=h/s
, let
v_{s}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=v(st^{\prime},sx^{\prime},sy^{\prime})
for any function
v
, then

(3.5)

(\Box v)_{s}=\frac{1}{s^{2}}\Box_{s}v_{s}\,\,\text{with }\,\,\Box_{s}=-\partial^{2}_{t^{\prime}}+\partial^{2}_{x^{\prime}}+s^{2}R(sx^{\prime},sy^{\prime},s^{-1}D_{y^{\prime}})\,.

Set
b=a/s
,
0<b\leq c_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{b,s,\hbar}=s^{d}(\mathcal{P}_{a,h})_{s}
; then one may apply the Melrose-Sjöstrand theorem to
\mathcal{P}_{b,s,\hbar}
to obtain
\mathcal{P}_{b,s,\hbar}\in O(\hbar^{\infty})
for
0\leq x^{\prime}\leq b
,
|y^{\prime}|\leq c_{0}
and
t^{\prime}=1
, when
s\leq T_{0}
and
T_{0}
is small enough. In fact,
\mathcal{P}_{b,s,\hbar}
is a parametrix for
\Box_{s}
, and
\Box_{s}
is smooth in
s
and for
s
small enough,
\Box_{s}
is close to the usual wave operator
-\partial^{2}_{t^{\prime}}+\partial^{2}_{x^{\prime}}+\Delta_{y^{\prime}}
(recall that we picked boundary normal coordinates, and
R_{0}(y,\eta)=|\eta|^{2}+O(|y|)
). If we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{b,s}
the Green function of
\Box_{s}
,

(3.6)

\Box_{s}\mathcal{G}_{b,s}=0\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\mathcal{G}_{b,s}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega,

with
\mathcal{G}_{b,s}|_{t^{\prime}=0}=\delta_{(b,0)}
and
\partial_{t^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{b,s}|_{t^{\prime}=0}=0
, then we have
\mathcal{P}_{b,s,\hbar}={\varkappa}(\hbar D_{t^{\prime}})\mathcal{Q}(sx^{\prime},sy^{\prime},\hbar D_{y^{\prime}})\mathcal{G}_{b,s}
and its wave front set is described by the Melrose-Sjöstrand theorem. To complete our proof, we need to check the following property of an optical ray
\sigma\in[0,1]\rightarrow\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau}_{s}(\sigma)
: if it starts at
\sigma=0
from
(b,0,\xi_{0},\eta_{0})
with
\xi^{2}_{0}+R(sb,0;\eta_{0})=1
and if
|\eta_{0}|\geq c_{1}>0
for some constant
c_{1}
, then, if
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{\tau}_{s}(\sigma)=(x^{\prime}(\sigma),y^{\prime}(\sigma),\xi^{\prime}(\sigma),\eta^{\prime}(\sigma))
, there exists
c_{0}
such that
|y^{\prime}(1)|\geq c_{0}>0
. Note that
(y^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})(\sigma)
are solutions to the Hamilton-Jacobi equations
\partial_{\sigma}y^{\prime}=\partial_{\eta^{\prime}}R_{s}
, and
\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\prime}=-\partial_{y^{\prime}}R_{s}
, with
y^{\prime}(0)=b
,
\eta^{\prime}(0)=\eta_{0}
,
R_{s}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=s^{2}R(sx^{\prime},xy^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}/s)
. For some
c>0
small,
|y^{\prime}(\sigma)|\geq 2c_{1}\sigma-cs\sigma
for all
\sigma\in[0,1]
, and if
T_{0}
is small enough, we get the lower bound
|y^{\prime}(1)|\geq c_{0}>0
for some
c_{0}>0
.
∎

3.1.
Number of waves that contribute in
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}

We further localize our parametrix
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}
: let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
be even,
\phi=1
on
[-1,1]
and
\phi=0
outside
(-3/2,3/2)
, set
\chi_{1}=\phi-\phi(2\cdot)
, then define (see (
2.31
)), for any dyadic
\gamma
(i.e.
1/\gamma\in 2^{{\mathbb{N}}}
) such that
\gamma\leq\epsilon_{0}
,

(3.7)

K_{\omega,\gamma}(f)(t,x,y):=\int e^{it\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)}G(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi^{\sharp}(\omega)q^{1/6}(\eta)\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\chi_{1}\Big(\frac{\omega}{\gamma|\eta|^{2/3}}\Big)\hat{f}\Big(\eta,\frac{\omega}{h^{1/3}}\Big)\,d\eta

as well as
\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}
by replacing
K_{\omega}
with
K_{\omega,\gamma}
in (
2.51
).
We just reduced the sum over
k
to
k
’s such that
k\sim\gamma^{3/2}/h
, where
h^{2/3-\epsilon}\lesssim\gamma\leq\epsilon_{0}
. Then,

\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)=\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}(t,x,y)
, where the sum is intended to be dyadic
\gamma^{\prime}s
with
\gamma<\epsilon_{0}
.
For
\gamma\ll a
, the corresponding
\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}
is irrelevant, as the phase of
g_{h,a}
is non-stationary. Using (
2.52
) or (
2.30
),

(3.8)

\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}(t,x,y)=\langle\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}e^{-iNL(\omega)},{\chi^{\flat}}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,a})(t,x,y)\rangle_{\omega}.

We will deal successively with
\gamma\sim a
and
4a\leq\gamma<1
. Heuristically, the first case contains ”tangent” initial directions (worst scenario); the second case contains all ”almost transverse” directions and provide less important contributions (even after summing up in
\gamma
). We write

(3.9)

\displaystyle V_{N,\gamma}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\int e^{-iNL(\omega)}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,a})(t,x,y)d\omega

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{2\pi h^{d+1}}\int e^{\frac{i}{h}(t\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)+\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)-\Phi(a,0,\theta,\alpha,s)-NhL(h^{-2/3}\alpha))}\chi_{1}(\alpha/(|\theta|^{2/3}\gamma))\\
{}\times{\chi^{\flat}}(\alpha/\epsilon_{0}){\chi^{\sharp}}(\alpha/h^{2/3})\chi(s)p_{h}(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)\tilde{q}_{h}(\theta,\alpha,s)ds\,d\theta d{\sigma}d\alpha\,,\end{multlined}

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N\in{\mathbb{Z}}}V_{N,\gamma}(t,x,y)\,,

where the symbol of
V_{N,\gamma}
(the same for every
N
) is of order
0
.

Lemma 3.3
.

At fixed
|t|\lesssim T_{0}
, the significant contributions in the sum (
3.12
) defining
\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}
come from
|N|\lesssim|t|/\sqrt{\gamma}
:

(3.13)

\sum_{|N|\geq 4|t|/\sqrt{\gamma}}V_{N,\gamma}(t,x,y)=O(h^{\infty})\,.

The proof of the lemma reproduces that of Proposition
2.14
; the maximum number of integrals that provide non-trivial contributions in the sum over
N
is
1/\sqrt{a}
when
\gamma\sim a
. Observe that Proposition
2.14
tells us that for
t=0
, only the
N=0
term may contribute.

At fixed
t\geq C\gamma^{1/2}
, we can further bound the cardinal of those
N
that contribute significantly among the
C|t|\gamma^{-1/2}
which are left. We introduce a few notations before stating a sharp bound of the number of waves that can overlap when
t/\sqrt{\gamma}
is large : let
\mathcal{N}(x,y,t)
be the set of
N
with significant contributions (e.g., we have a stationary point for the phase in all variables),

(3.14)

\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)=\{N\in\mathbb{Z},(\exists)(\sigma,s,\alpha,\theta)\text{ such that }\nabla_{(\sigma,s,\alpha,\theta)}\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}=0\},

where
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
, the phase function of
V_{N,\gamma}
for the large parameter
1/h
, is defined as follows

(3.15)

\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}:=t\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)+\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)-\Phi(a,0,\theta,\alpha,s)-\frac{4}{3}N\alpha^{3/2}+NhB_{L}(\alpha^{3/2}/h).

Let
(t,x,y)
such that
\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)\neq\emptyset
and assume without loss of generality that
t>0
and
t/\sqrt{\gamma}
is large. As we shall see below, at a critical point
(\sigma,s,\alpha,\theta)
of
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
,

\frac{y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\vartheta)}{|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\vartheta)|}=-{\vartheta}+O(\alpha),\quad\frac{|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\vartheta)|}{t}=1+O(\alpha),

and on the support of the symbol
\chi_{1}
,
\alpha\sim\gamma\ll 1
. Therefore, as
B_{0}(y,\omega)=O(|y|^{2})
and
\nabla B_{0}(y,\omega)=O(|y|^{2})
, if
\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)\neq\emptyset
then we must have
\frac{|y|}{t}=1+O(|y|)
.

Consider first
d>2
. Let
c
satisfy
4\gamma<c<\frac{1}{16}
and such that
1-c\leq\frac{|y|}{t}\leq 1+c
(notice that such
c
does exist since otherwise
\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)=\emptyset
). As
B_{2}(y,\vartheta)=O(|y|^{2})
,
|y|\lesssim T_{0}
with
T_{0}
sufficiently small, there exists
\tilde{\vartheta}=\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\setminus\{0\}
such that, with
\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}_{\theta}=\nabla_{\theta}-\frac{\theta}{|\theta|}(\frac{\theta}{|\theta|}\cdot\nabla)
,

(3.16)

\tilde{\vartheta}=-\frac{y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\tilde{\vartheta})}{|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\tilde{\vartheta})|}\\
{}-2\Big(\frac{|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\tilde{\vartheta})|}{t}-1\Big)\Big[\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\tilde{\vartheta})}{q(\tilde{\vartheta})}-\frac{3\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}B_{2}(y,\tilde{\vartheta})}{2t(1-B_{2}(y,\tilde{\vartheta})/t)}\Big]\,,

as a fixed point of a continuous map from a ball to a ball. In fact, as
B_{0}(y,\theta)
(resp.
B_{2}(y,\theta)
) is homogeneous of degree
1
(resp.
0
) in
\theta
, so is
\nabla B_{0}
(of degree
0
), hence the right hand side in (
3.16
) does not depend on
|\tilde{\vartheta}|
. Uniqueness follows by taking differences and using smallness of
B_{0}
. Moreover, for sufficiently small
c
and
T_{0}
we have
|\tilde{\vartheta}|\in[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]
.
As we shall see below (in (
3.68
) from Lemma
3.10
),
\tilde{\vartheta}(t,,y)
is an approximation (modulo
O(\gamma^{3/2}/t)
terms) of the critical point
\vartheta_{\theta}
of the phase
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
.
Let
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
be such that

\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then
1-c\leq\frac{|y^{\prime}|}{t^{\prime}}\leq 1+c
and there exists
\tilde{\vartheta}(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
solution to (
3.16
) with
(t,y)
replaced by
(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
We now define a cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}(t,x,y)
as the set of
(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle|(t^{\prime}-B_{2}(y^{\prime},\tilde{\vartheta}(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})))-(t-B_{2}(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y)))|\leq r_{0}\sqrt{\gamma},

(3.18)

\displaystyle|x^{\prime}(1+\ell(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t^{\prime},y^{\prime})))-x(1+\ell(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y)))|\leq r_{0}\gamma,

(3.19)

\displaystyle||y^{\prime}+\nabla B_{0}(y^{\prime},\tilde{\vartheta}(t^{\prime},y^{\prime}))|-t^{\prime}-|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y))|+t|\leq r_{0}\gamma^{3/2},

and let, with
\mathcal{N}
defined in (
3.14
),

(3.20)

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d>2}(t,x,y)=\cup_{\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}(t,x,y)}\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

For
d=2
, replace
\nabla B_{0}(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y))
by
B_{0}(y)
,
B_{2}(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y))
by
B_{2}(y)
and
\ell(y,\tilde{\vartheta}(t,y))
by
\ell(y)
to define
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d=2}(t,x,y)
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For any
d\geq 2
, the following optimal upper bound holds for
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d}
defined in (
3.20
)

(3.21)

\left|\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d}(t,x,y)\right|\lesssim O(1)+\gamma^{-1/2}{|t|}(\gamma^{3/2}/h)^{-2}\,.

Remark 3.5
.

A particular case of the two dimensional version of Proposition
3.4
has been proved in
[
9
, Lemma 2.17, Lemma 2.18]
in the case
a\gg h^{4/7}
(and without the
\gamma
cut-off), where we proved
\left|\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d=2}(t,x,y)\right|
was bounded by a constant and that
\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)\subset[1,t/(2\sqrt{a})+N_{0}]
, with
N_{0}
being an absolute constant. For
a\sim\gamma\gg h^{4/7}
and
|t|\lesssim 1
, one easily sees that
|t|/(\gamma^{1/2}(\gamma^{3}/h^{2}))=O(1)
. Indeed,
\gamma^{7/2}/h^{2}\gg 1
, hence the right hand side term in (
3.21
) is
O(1)
. Therefore, if
\gamma\gg h^{4/7}
, we only get non-trivial contributions from an uniformly bounded number of waves at a fixed
t
.
The case
\gamma\gg h^{2/3}
was recently dealt with in
[
12
]
where Propositions
3.4
and
3.13
were proved in the 2D Friedlander model domain. Compared to
[
12
]
, there are significant additional difficulties with angles in the higher dimensional case (even in the model situation !)

Proof.

We first provide a proof in all dimensions
d\geq 2
for the model case, with
\Delta_{M}
: a parametrix of the wave equation reads as (
3.12
), where
V_{N,\gamma}
has symbol
\chi_{1}(\alpha/(\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}))\chi^{\flat}(\alpha/\varepsilon_{0}){\chi^{\sharp}}(\alpha/h^{2/3})\chi(s)
and phase function
\Phi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}
given by

\Phi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}(t,x,y,\sigma,s,\alpha,\theta):=t\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)+\Phi_{M}(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)-\Phi_{M}(a,0,\theta,\alpha,s)-\frac{4}{3}N\alpha^{3/2}+NhB_{L}(\alpha^{3/2}/h),

where
\Phi_{M}(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=y\cdot\theta+\frac{\sigma^{3}}{3}+\sigma(xq^{1/3}(\theta)-\alpha)
; note that the only difference between
\Phi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}
and
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
comes from the additional terms
\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)\Gamma(x,y,\frac{\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)}{\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)},\frac{\theta}{\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)})-\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)\Gamma(a,0,\frac{sq^{1/3}(\theta)}{\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)},\frac{\theta}{\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)})
(difference between
\Phi_{M}
and
\Phi
). In the model case, without the additional phase function
\Gamma
, we rescale
x=\gamma X
,
t=\sqrt{\gamma}T
,
y=\sqrt{\gamma}Y
, and our cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}(t,x,y)
and
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d\geq 2}
simplify to
\mathcal{N}^{1,M}_{d}(T,X,Y):=\cup_{\mathcal{C}^{M}_{\gamma}(T,X,Y)}\mathcal{N}^{M}(T^{\prime},X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
and

\mathcal{C}^{M}_{\gamma}(T,X,Y)=\{(T^{\prime},X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\,\,:\,\,|Y^{\prime}-Y|\leq r_{0},|X-X^{\prime}|<r_{0},||Y^{\prime}|-T^{\prime}-|Y|+T|<r_{0}\gamma\}\,.

Note that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}^{M}_{\gamma}(T,X,Y)
implies
||Y^{\prime}|-|Y||\leq r_{0}
,
|Y|\Big|\frac{Y^{\prime}}{|Y^{\prime}|}-\frac{Y}{|Y|}\Big|\leq 2r_{0}
and
|T^{\prime}-T|\leq r_{0}
.
We also rescale
\sigma=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}\Sigma
,
\alpha=\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}A
,
s=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}S
and we let
\lambda_{\gamma}=\frac{\gamma^{3/2}}{h}
.
Define our new phase to be (with large parameter
1/h
replaced by
\lambda_{\gamma}
)

(3.22)

\Psi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}(T,X,Y,\Sigma,S,A,\theta)=|\theta|\Big(\frac{Y\cdot\vartheta+T\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}{\gamma}+\frac{\Sigma^{3}}{3}+\Sigma(Xq^{1/3}(\vartheta)-A)\\
-\frac{S^{3}}{3}-S(\frac{a}{\gamma}q^{1/3}(\vartheta)-A)-\frac{4}{3}NA^{3/2}\Big)+\frac{N}{\lambda_{\gamma}}B_{L}(|\theta|\lambda_{\gamma}A^{3/2})\,.

The phase
\Psi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}
defines a Lagrangian
\Lambda^{M}_{N}
, which is described by
\nabla_{A,S,\Sigma,\theta}\Psi^{M}_{N,a\gamma}=0
:

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{Tq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}{2\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}-(\Sigma-S)-2NA^{1/2}(1-\frac{3}{4}{B_{L}}^{\prime}(|\theta|\lambda_{\gamma}A^{3/2}))=0,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}+Xq^{1/3}(\vartheta)-A=0,\quad S^{2}+\frac{a}{\gamma}q^{1/3}(\vartheta)-A=0,

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y+T\vartheta\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}+\gamma\Big(\frac{TAq^{1/3}(\vartheta)}{\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}+(\Sigma X-S\frac{a}{\gamma})\Big)\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\vartheta)}{3q^{2/3}(\vartheta)}\\
{}+\frac{2}{3}\gamma\vartheta(S^{3}-\Sigma^{3})=\frac{4}{3}\gamma NA^{3/2}(1-\frac{3}{4}{B_{L}}^{\prime}(|\theta|\lambda_{\gamma}A^{3/2}))\vartheta\,,\end{multlined}

where we used the second and third equation for substitution in the last one.
We recover
[
9
, formula (2.13) to (2.15)]
when
d=2
, with small adjustments due to our (more complicated) phase construction there. Assume, without loss of generality, that
T>0
, then eliminating
N
between (
3.23
) and (
3.1
),

(3.27)

\frac{Y}{T}=-\Big(1+\frac{\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}{1+\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}-\frac{\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}{3\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}+\frac{2\gamma}{3T}(A(\Sigma-S)+(S^{3}-\Sigma^{3}))\Big)\vartheta\\
{}-\Big(\frac{\gamma Aq^{1/3}(\vartheta)}{\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}}+\frac{\gamma}{T}\Big((\Sigma X-S\frac{a}{\gamma})\Big)\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\vartheta)}{3q^{2/3}(\vartheta)}

Using that
\vartheta\cdot\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\vartheta)=0
, we compute
|Y|^{2}/T^{2}
and expand its square root to get

(3.28)

\quad\quad\frac{|Y|}{T}-1=\frac{\gamma}{6}Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)+\frac{2\gamma}{3T}\big(A(\Sigma-S)+S^{3}-{\Sigma^{3}}\big)+{\gamma^{2}}\mathcal{E}\,,

where
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(1/T,\Sigma X-S\frac{a}{\gamma},A,S,\Sigma,\vartheta)
is a smooth function of its arguments (that may be computed explicitly, although irrelevant). We then compute

(3.29)

\frac{Y}{|Y|}=-\vartheta-\frac{\gamma A}{3}\Big(\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\vartheta)}{q^{1/3}(\vartheta)}\Big)+\frac{\gamma}{T}(\Sigma X-S\frac{a}{\gamma})\frac{\mathord{\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 4.16667pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.34029pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.88199pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\nabla}}}}q(\vartheta)}{3q^{2/3}(\vartheta)}+\gamma^{2}\vec{\mathcal{E}}\,,

where
\vec{\mathcal{E}}=\vec{\mathcal{E}}(1/T,\Sigma X-S\frac{a}{\gamma},A,S,\Sigma,\vartheta)
is an explicit smooth, vector-valued function. Later we will use
O(\gamma^{2})\lesssim O(\gamma/T)
, as
T\sqrt{\gamma}=O(1)
. We now estimate the distance between any two elements of
\mathcal{N}^{1,M}_{d}(T,X,Y)
. Pick
(T,X,Y)
with
T>0
, and let
N_{j}\in\mathcal{N}^{1,M}_{d}(T,X,Y)
,
j\in\{1,2\}
. There exist
(T_{j},X_{j},Y_{j})\in\mathcal{C}^{M}_{\gamma}

and there exist
(\theta_{j}=|\theta_{j}|\vartheta_{j},A_{j},\Sigma_{j},S_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
,
A_{j}
close to
1
, such that (
3.23
), (
3.24
), (
3.27
) hold.
Taking the difference between (
3.23
) for
j=1,2
and using (
3.24
) (
\frac{(\Sigma_{j}-S_{j})}{A_{j}^{1/2}}=O(1)
) and
\gamma T_{j}=O(\sqrt{\gamma}t_{j})=O(\sqrt{\gamma})
,

(3.30)

N_{1}-N_{2}=\frac{3}{4}\Big(N_{1}B_{L}^{\prime}(|\theta_{1}|\lambda_{\gamma}A_{1}^{3/2})-N_{2}B_{L}^{\prime}(|\theta_{2}|\lambda_{\gamma}A_{2}^{3/2})\Big)-\frac{\Sigma_{1}-S_{1}}{2\sqrt{A_{1}}}+\frac{\Sigma_{2}-S_{2}}{2\sqrt{A_{2}}}\\
+\frac{T_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})}{4A_{1}^{1/2}\sqrt{1+\gamma A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})}}-\frac{T_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})}{4A_{2}^{1/2}\sqrt{1+\gamma A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})}}\\
=O\Big(\frac{N_{1}+N_{2}}{\lambda_{\gamma}^{2}}\Big)+O(1)+\frac{T_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})}{4A_{1}^{1/2}}-\frac{T_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})}{4A_{2}^{1/2}}\,.

Remark 3.6
.

The first term in the first line of (
3.30
) behaves like
(|N_{1}|+|N_{2}|)/\lambda_{\gamma}^{2}
, using that
B^{\prime}(\theta\lambda A^{3/2})\sim-\frac{b_{1}}{\theta^{2}\lambda^{2}A^{3}}
and
\theta,A\sim 1
. We cannot take advantage of the difference, since each term
N_{j}B^{\prime}(\cdot)
corresponds to some
\theta_{j},A_{j}
(close to
1
) and, although the difference between
A_{j}
turns out to be
O(1/T)
, we do not have any better information about the difference between
|\theta_{j}|
than bounded by a small constant on the support of
\chi_{1}
. Therefore the bound
(N_{1}+N_{2})/\lambda_{\gamma}^{2}
for the terms involving
B^{\prime}_{L}
in (
3.30
) is sharp. As
N_{j}\sim T_{j}
, and
|T_{j}-T|\leq 1
, this contribution is of order
|T|/\lambda^{2}_{\gamma}
.

We are reduced to proving that the following difference (from (
3.30
)) is
O(1)
. Write

(3.31)

\frac{T_{1}}{A_{1}^{1/2}}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-\frac{T_{2}}{A_{2}^{1/2}}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})=\frac{(T_{1}-T_{2})}{A_{1}^{1/2}}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})+\frac{T_{2}}{\sqrt{A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})}}(q(\vartheta_{1})-q(\vartheta_{2}))\\
+T_{2}q(\vartheta_{2})(\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})}}).

As
|T_{1}-T_{2}|\leq|T_{1}-T|+|T_{2}-T|\leq 2r_{0}
, it remains to prove

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(T,X,Y)
be fixed, let
(T_{j},X_{j},Y_{j})\in\mathcal{C}^{M}_{\gamma}
and let
(\theta_{j},A_{j},\Sigma_{j},S_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2\}
with
A_{j}
close to
1
such that (
3.23
), (
3.24
) and (
3.27
) hold, then

(3.32)

T|\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}|\lesssim 1,\quad T|A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})|\lesssim 1.

Proof.

When
T
is not too large, (
3.32
) immediately follows. We consider
T
sufficiently large. For
T_{j},Y_{j},A_{j},\vartheta_{j}
, (
3.28
) and (
3.29
) hold.
Taking difference between (
3.29
) for
j=1
and
j=2
,

(3.33)

-\frac{Y_{1}}{|Y_{1}|}+\frac{Y_{2}}{|Y_{2}|}=(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2})\Big(1-\frac{2}{3}\gamma A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})\Big)-\frac{2}{3}\vartheta_{2}\gamma(A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2}))\\
+\gamma(A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2}))\frac{\nabla q(\vartheta_{2})}{q(\vartheta_{2})}+\gamma A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})\Big(\frac{\nabla q(\vartheta_{1})}{q(\vartheta_{1})}-\frac{\nabla q(\vartheta_{2})}{q(\vartheta_{2})}\Big)+O(\frac{\gamma}{T}),

where we used that
O(\frac{\gamma}{T_{1}})=O(\frac{\gamma}{T_{2}})=O(\frac{\gamma}{T})
. As
A_{j}
’s stay close to
1
and
\vartheta_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
, we get

(3.34)

(\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2})(1+O(\gamma))=\Big(-\frac{Y_{1}}{|Y_{1}|}+\frac{Y_{2}}{|Y_{2}|}\Big)+O(\gamma)\,.

Using
\big|-\frac{Y_{1}}{|Y_{1}|}+\frac{Y_{2}}{|Y_{2}|}\big|=\big|-\frac{y_{1}}{|y_{1}|}+\frac{y_{2}}{|y_{2}|}\big|\leq 2\frac{r_{0}}{|Y|}
and
T|\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}|\lesssim t(2r_{0}+O(\sqrt{\gamma}))\lesssim 2r_{0}
, we obtain the first inequality in (
3.32
). Taking now the difference between (
3.28
) for
j=1,2
,

6\Big(\frac{|Y_{1}|}{T_{1}}-\frac{|Y_{2}|}{T_{2}}\Big)=\gamma(A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2}))+O(\frac{\gamma}{T}),

and using
|(|Y_{j}|-T_{j})-(|Y|-T)|\leq r_{0}\gamma
,
|T_{1}-T_{2}|\leq 2r_{0}
and (
3.28
) with
j=2
yields

(3.35)

\displaystyle T|A_{1}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{1})-A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6T}{\gamma}\Big|\frac{|Y_{1}|}{T_{1}}-\frac{|Y_{2}|}{T_{2}}\Big|+O(1)

(3.36)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6T}{\gamma}\frac{|(|Y_{1}|-T_{1})-(|Y_{2}|-T_{2})|}{T_{1}}+\frac{6T}{\gamma}\Big(\frac{|Y_{2}|}{T_{2}}-1\Big)\frac{(T_{2}-T_{1})}{T_{1}}+O(1)

(3.37)

\displaystyle\leq 12r_{0}\frac{T}{T_{1}}+2r_{0}\frac{T}{T_{1}}\Big(A_{2}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{2})+O(\frac{1}{T_{2}})\Big)+O(1)=O(1),

where, again, on the support of the symbol
\chi_{1}
,
A_{j}
is close to
1
. The proof of Lemma
3.7
is complete and, combined with (
3.31
) and (
3.30
), this yields (
3.21
) for the model operator
\Delta_{M}
.
∎

We now proceed with the general case, following the same steps as above. We first deal with the most complicated situation
d>2
. We also rescale
variables as follows
\sigma=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}\Sigma
,
\alpha=\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}A
,
s=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}S
and let
\lambda_{\gamma}=\frac{\gamma^{3/2}}{h}
. With
\vartheta=\theta/|\theta|
we get
q(\theta)=|\theta|^{2}q(\vartheta)
,
\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)=\tau_{q}(\gamma A|\theta|^{2/3},\theta)=|\theta|\sqrt{1+\gamma Aq^{2/3}(\vartheta)}=:|\theta|\tau_{q}(\gamma A,\vartheta)
. We retain space-time variables
(t,x,y)
as our phase function is no longer homogeneous in
y
.
Recall that

(3.38)

\Phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=\Phi_{M}(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)+\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)(B_{\Gamma}(y,\theta/\tau_{q})+xA_{\Gamma}(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/\tau_{q},\theta/\tau_{q})\,,

where
\tau_{q}=\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)=\sqrt{|\theta|^{2}+\alpha q^{2/3}(\theta)}
. Let

(3.39)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{M,\gamma}(x,y,\vartheta,A,\Sigma):

\displaystyle=|\theta|^{-1}\Phi_{M}(x,y,\theta,\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}A,\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}\Sigma)

(3.40)

\displaystyle=y\cdot\vartheta+\gamma^{3/2}\Big(\frac{\Sigma^{3}}{3}+\Sigma(\frac{x}{\gamma}q^{1/3}(\vartheta)-A)\Big).

Set
\tilde{\Phi}_{\gamma}(x,y,\vartheta,A,\Sigma):=|\theta|^{-1}\Phi(x,y,\theta,\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}A,\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}\Sigma)
, then, using the homogeneity in
|\theta|
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}_{\gamma}(x,y,\vartheta,A,\Sigma):=\begin{multlined}\tilde{\Phi}_{M,\gamma}(x,y,\vartheta,A,\Sigma)+\tau_{q}(\gamma A,\vartheta)\Big[B_{\Gamma}(y,\vartheta/\tau_{q}(\gamma A,\vartheta))\\
