. If we decide to ignore the integral with respect to
r\in\text{supp}\varkappa
, we can immediately bound the sum of integrals with

(4.13)

\|\mathcal{P}^{1}_{h,a}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{h^{1/3}}{h^{d}}\Big(\frac{h}{t}\Big)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{h^{-\epsilon}\leq k\leq K_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{\omega_{k}^{1/2}}\frac{1}{\omega_{k}^{1/2}}\lesssim h^{-d}\Big(\frac{h}{t}\Big)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}(hK_{\epsilon})^{1/3}\,,

and as
K_{\epsilon}\leq h^{-1/4+\epsilon}
,
(hK_{\epsilon})^{1/3}\leq h^{1/4+\epsilon/3}
which is not optimal (a similar crude bound was obtained in
[
9
]
in that regime.) To obtain sharper bounds when
t\geq K_{\epsilon}^{-1}
,
h^{-\epsilon}\leq k\leq K_{\epsilon}
, decompose
{Ai}(-z)=A_{+}(z)+A_{-}(z)
, with
A_{\pm}(z)=\Psi(e^{\mp i\pi/3}z)e^{\mp\frac{2}{3}iz^{3/2}}
and

4\pi^{3/2}|\Psi(z)|\sim|z|^{-1/4}
for large
z
. Then there are four different phases in
K_{\omega_{k}}(g_{h,a,1})
, where
\pm_{1}
and
\pm_{2}
mean idependent signs,

\frac{1}{h}\Big(t\tau_{q}(h^{2/3}\omega_{k},\theta)+\psi(x,y,\theta,\omega_{k})\pm_{1}\frac{2}{3}\zeta(x,y,\theta,h^{2/3}\omega_{k})^{3/2}\pm_{2}\frac{2}{3}\zeta(a,0,\theta,h^{2/3}\omega_{k})^{3/2}\Big)

and a symbol which behaves like
{h^{-(d-1)-2/3}}q^{1/3}(\theta)\varkappa(\theta)|\zeta(x,y,\theta/h,\omega_{k})|^{-1}|\zeta(a,0,\theta/h,\omega_{k})|^{-1/4}
, where
h^{-2/3}\zeta(x,y,\theta,h^{2/3}\omega_{k})=\zeta(x,y,\theta/h,\omega_{k})=\omega_{k}-xq^{1/3}(\theta)/h^{2/3}e_{0}(x,y,\theta/h,\omega_{k})\geq\omega_{k}/2
and also
\zeta(a,0,\theta/h,\omega_{k})=\omega_{k}-aq^{1/3}(\theta/h)e_{0}(a,0,\theta/h,\omega_{k})\geq\omega_{k}/2
. We prove that stationary phase applies with the same ”large parameter”
th^{2/3}\omega_{k}/h
but a new symbol.
As in (
4.10
), we find

t-|y+\nabla B_{0}(y,\vartheta_{-})|+rO(|y|^{2})O(h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k})+\frac{2}{3}r^{-\frac{2}{3}}h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k}q^{\frac{2}{3}}(\vartheta_{-})\Big(t+B_{0}(y,\vartheta_{-})-B_{2}(y,\vartheta_{-})-x\mu(y,\vartheta_{-})\Big)\\
{}+O((h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k})^{2})\pm_{1}x\partial_{r}(q^{\frac{1}{3}}(r\vartheta_{-})e_{0})\sqrt{\zeta(x,y,r\vartheta_{-},h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k})}\pm_{2}a\partial_{r}(q^{\frac{1}{3}}(r\vartheta_{-})e_{0})\sqrt{\zeta(a,0,r\vartheta_{-},h^{\frac{2}{3}}\omega_{k})}=0.

The second derivative is

-\frac{4}{9}r^{-5/3}h^{2/3}\omega_{k}q^{2/3}(\vartheta_{-})(t+O(|y|^{2}))+O((h^{2/3}\omega_{k})^{2}))\mp_{1}xO(\sqrt{h^{2/3}\omega_{k}})(1+O((h^{2/3}\omega_{k})^{2})\\
{}\mp_{2}aO(\sqrt{h^{2/3}\omega_{k}})(1+O((h^{2/3}\omega_{k})^{2})),

and for
t\geq K_{\epsilon}^{-1}
,
h^{-\epsilon}\leq k\leq K_{\epsilon}
, the main contribution comes from the first term : indeed,
t\sqrt{h^{2/3}\omega_{k}}\geq K_{\epsilon}^{-1}h^{1/3(1-\epsilon)}\geq h^{7/12-4\epsilon/3}\gg h^{2/3-\epsilon}
, hence
t(h^{2/3}\omega_{k})\gg\max\{x,a\}O(\sqrt{h^{2/3}\omega_{k}})
for all
x,a\lesssim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
. We then check that
th^{2/3}\omega_{k}/h
is a large parameter for
t>K_{\epsilon}^{-1}
(recall that in Lemma
4.4
we had
t\geq h^{\epsilon}\gg K_{\epsilon}^{-1}
): indeed,
t\omega_{k}\geq K_{\epsilon}^{-1}h^{-2\epsilon/3}\geq h^{1/4-5\epsilon/3}\gg h^{1/3}
. We find

(4.14)

\|\mathcal{P}^{1}_{h,a}(t,x,y)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{h^{1/3}}{h^{d}}\Big(\frac{h}{t}\Big)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sum_{1\leq k\lesssim K_{\epsilon}}\frac{1}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})\omega_{k}^{1/4+1/4}}\Big(\frac{h^{1/3}}{t\omega_{k}}\Big)^{1/2}\sim h^{-d}\Big(\frac{h}{t}\Big)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\Big(\frac{h}{t}\Big)^{1/2}\log{h^{-1}}\,,

where we use the symbol of both factors
A_{\pm}
to get
|\zeta|^{-1/4}<2/\omega_{k}^{1/4}
. We proved (
4.1
) and therefore Theorem
3.1
.
∎

5.
Strichartz estimates

Standard duality and interpolation arguments would lead to Theorem
1.5
with
\upgamma(d)=1/4
if one only uses dispersion formula (
1.3
). We aim at improving on this straightforward application of our dispersion estimate, combining three ingredients, where the first two were already proven earlier: in our estimates from Propositions
3.21
,
3.22
,
3.23
and
4.1
, we either have better decay or additional (small) factors of
a
or
\gamma
; we also have the usual dispersion on time intervals with lengh less than
\sqrt{\gamma}
, in the sum of reflections regime (corresponding to dispersion on the
V_{0}
term in the expansion over
N
); and finally, we claim that the additional cut-off that we introduced to localize
h^{2/3}\omega\sim\gamma
is not only a useful technical device but also essentially commutes with the flow itself. Assuming these facts, one can then prove Strichartz estimates at fixed
\gamma
: on intervals of length one, with the
\upgamma(d)=1/4
loss but with a constant that depends on a positive power of
\gamma
; on intervals of size
\sqrt{\gamma}
, one has the usual Strichartz estimates, with
\upgamma(d)=0
, and by iteration, one has a corresponding Strichartz estimate on time length one but with a constant that depends on a negative power of
\gamma
; by interpolation, one recovers a Strichartz estimate, at fixed
\gamma
, that improves upon
\upgamma(d)=1/4
, and then summation over
\gamma
yields the theorem, with strict inequality on
\upgamma(d)
because of that last summation over
\gamma
. Such a strategy was implemented in
[
11
]
and is facilitated there because the
\gamma
localization commutes with the wave flow. Moreover, a refined analysis of the dispersion estimates around the exceptional times that force
\upgamma(d)=1/4
allows for a better Strichartz estimate to interpolate with, thus yielding a better result on the 2D model. We expect such result to generalize to the 2D general convex case, while higher dimensions will require additional arguments related to the space-time localization of swallowtails (which can no longer be a set of discrete, exceptional, times). As such, we believe that Theorem
1.5
is of interest as it illustrates, in a relatively simple way, that the rate of dispersion does tell the whole story as far as Strichartz estimates are concerned.

We now turn to the details: we relabel
\mathcal{P}_{h}(t,x,y,a,b)
our parametrix but with source
(a,b)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and similarly
\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}(t,x,y,a,b)
where an additional cut-off in
\alpha
was inserted. Inhomogeneous Strichartz estimates follow from estimating the inhomogeneous operator
P_{h}
(and/or
P_{h,\gamma}
with obvious notations)

P_{h}f(t,x,y)=\int_{s}\mathcal{P}_{h}(t-s,x,y,a,b)f(s,a,b)\,dadbds\,.

We remark that, with such notations, the homogeneous operator approximating the half-wave flow with data
f_{0}(a,b)
at time
t=0
is

(W_{h}f_{0})(t,x,y)=P_{h}(\delta_{s=0}f_{0}(a,b))(t,x,y)\,,

and we define similarly
W_{h,\gamma}
. Now, suppose we restrict ourselves to
{P}_{h}^{\flat}=\sum_{\gamma<h^{1/3}}{P}_{h,\gamma}
: for
\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}
with
\gamma<h^{1/3}
, we have
a^{1/4}h^{1/4}\lesssim h^{1/3}
and, collecting all bounds from Propositions
3.21
,
3.22
,
3.23
and
4.1
in our regime, we do have

(5.1)

|\mathcal{P}_{h}^{\flat}(t,x,y,a,b)|\lesssim\frac{1}{h^{d}}\Bigl(\frac{h}{t}\Bigr)^{(d-2)/2}\min\left(1,\Bigl(\frac{h}{t}\Bigr)^{1/3}\right)\,.

Commuting the wave flow and the
\alpha\sim\gamma
localization in the parametrix is an issue, to be adressed in Proposition
5.1
below; we already observe that
\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}
is an approximate solution to the wave equation, and as such, satisfies energy estimates. We then obtain inhomogeneous Strichartz estimates but with
\upgamma(d)=1/6
rather than
1/4
, after interpolation between (
5.1
) and energy estimates, followed by the argument from
[
14
]
for the endpoint (only required for
d\geq 4
) and a dependence on
h
dictated by scaling, which we encode with
\lesssim_{h}
:

(5.2)

\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{3d-4}{3d-7}}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{3d-4}{3d+3}}_{x}}\,,\quad\text{or},\,\text{for}\,d=3,\,\,\,\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{\frac{1}{2}5}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\|f\|_{L^{\frac{12}{7}}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}\,.

For now, we focus on a given
\gamma>h^{1/3}
.
First, we remark that
{P}_{h,\gamma}
satisfies inhomogeneous Strichartz estimates with
\upgamma(d)=1/4
, but with a constant related to
\gamma
(from the
a^{1/4}
,
\gamma^{1/4}
factors in (
3.129
), (
3.130
), (
3.131
))

(5.3)

\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{2d-3}{2d-7}}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{\frac{1}{2d-3}}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{2d-3}{2d+4}}_{x}}\,,\quad\text{or},\,\text{for}\,d=3,\,\,\,\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{\frac{8}{3}}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{\frac{1}{4}}\|f\|_{L^{\frac{8}{5}}_{t}L^{1}_{x}}\,.

This follows from performing the usual duality and interpolation argument directly on
P_{h,\gamma}
rather than
P_{h}
, using Proposition
5.1
to handle commutation of the localization with the flow.
At the same time,
W_{h,\gamma}
satisfies the usual short time dispersion (either from
[
2
]
or from (
3.128
) and close inspection of the
N=1
cases, especially Lemma
3.36
), hence homogeneous Strichartz estimates on a time interval of size
\gamma^{1/2}
(this may be seen as a direct consequence of short time Strichartz estimates proven in a more general context in
[
2
]
.) With the help of Proposition
5.1
, one may sum such
L^{2}_{t}
estimates over
\gamma^{-1/2}
intervals of length
\gamma^{-1/2}
, obtaining

\|W_{h,\gamma}f_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{d-1}{d-3}}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|f_{0}\|_{2}

and then revert to inhomogenous estimates,

(5.4)

\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{d-1}{d-3}}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2\frac{d-1}{d+1}}_{x}}\,.

(abusing the enpoint for
d=3
, for which exponents are to be shifted to avoid the forbidden endpoint). One then interpolates between (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) to retain
\gamma^{\varepsilon(q)}
, with
\varepsilon(q)>0
dictated by interpolation (to sum over
\gamma
later),

(5.5)

\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{\varepsilon(q)}\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}}\,,\quad\text{or},\,\text{for}\,d=3,\,\,\,\|P_{h,\gamma}f\|_{L^{\frac{12}{5-12\varepsilon}}_{t}L^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}_{x}}\lesssim_{h}\gamma^{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{\frac{12}{7+12\varepsilon}}_{t}L^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}_{x}}\,.

with
1/2-1/q>2d/((d-1)(2d-1))
for
d\geq 4
: this corresponds to
\upgamma(d)>1/4-1/4d
for
d\geq 3
. Taking advantage of
\gamma^{\varepsilon}
, we sum over dyadic
\gamma
’s to get estimates for
P^{\sharp}_{h}=\sum_{\gamma>h^{1/3}}P_{h,\gamma}
, and the resulting estimate is always worse than (
5.3
) for
P^{\flat}_{h}
, that is to say,
q>2(3d-4)/(3d-7)
. As such,
P_{h}=P^{\sharp}_{h}+P^{\flat}_{h}
satisfies the same set of estimates as
P^{\sharp}_{h}
, that is, Strichartz estimates with
\upgamma(d)>1/4-1/4d=1/6+(1/4)(1/3-1/d)
. In particular, for
d=3
, we obtain (except for the endpoint) the set of Strichartz estimates that would result from dispersion with a loss of only
1/6
, which is the loss resulting from cusp-like singularities in the wave front. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.5
, up to proving Proposition
5.1
.

It remains to handle the issue of localization with respect to
\alpha
in our parametrix. For this, we check that we do have a suitable form of the group property for the operator
W_{h,\gamma}(t)
: assume that
\tilde{W}_{h,\gamma}
is the same operator (with kernel denoted by
\mathcal{\tilde{P}}_{h,\gamma}
) but with a cut-off in
\alpha
, denoted
\tilde{\chi}_{1}
, which is the identity on the support of the cut-off
\chi_{1}
in
W_{h,\gamma}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

We have
\tilde{W}_{h,\gamma}(t_{1})\circ W_{h,\gamma}(t_{2})=W_{h,\gamma}(t_{1}+t_{2})
, modulo
O(h^{\infty})
. In terms of kernels,

(5.6)

\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y;{a,b})=\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}\mathcal{\tilde{P}}_{h,\gamma}(t_{1},x,y;{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}})\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}(t_{2},\tilde{x},\tilde{y};a,b)\,d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}+O(h^{\infty})\,.

Similarly, for
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
such that the corresponding cut-offs have disjoint support,
W_{h,\gamma_{1}}(t_{1})\circ W_{h,\gamma_{2}}(t_{2})=O(h^{\infty})
and

(5.7)

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma_{1}}(t_{1},x,y;{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}})\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma_{2}}(t_{2},\tilde{x},\tilde{y};a,b)\,d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=O(h^{\infty})\,.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}
is given by (
3.8
) with
K_{\omega,\gamma}
defined in (
3.7
). Then we rewrite

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}\mathcal{\tilde{P}}_{h,\gamma}(t_{1},x,y;{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}})\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma_{1}}(t_{2},\tilde{x},\tilde{y};a,b)\,d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=\sum_{N_{1},N_{2}}\int e^{-iN_{1}L(\omega)}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})J_{N_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y,\omega)d\omega,

where we set

\displaystyle J_{N_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y,\omega)=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}\tilde{K}_{\omega,\gamma_{1}}(g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})})(t_{1},x,y)F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}\,,

\displaystyle F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=

\displaystyle e^{-iN_{2}L(\tilde{\omega})}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\tilde{\omega}/\epsilon_{0})K_{\tilde{\omega},\gamma_{2}}(g_{h,(a,b)})(t_{2},\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\,,

and
\tilde{K}_{\omega,\gamma_{1}}(g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})})
has the same form as (
3.7
) but with a cut-off
\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})
instead of
\chi_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})
, supported near
1
and equal to
1
on the support of
\chi_{1}
.

From (
3.7
),
F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
expands as

\int G(\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})\chi^{\#}(\tilde{\omega})q^{1/6}(\tilde{\eta})\varkappa(h\tilde{\eta})\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\eta}))\chi_{1}(\frac{\tilde{\omega}}{\gamma_{2}|\tilde{\eta}|^{2/3}})e^{-iN_{2}L(\tilde{\omega})+it_{2}\tau_{q}(\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\eta})}\hat{g}_{h,(a,b)}(\tilde{\eta},\frac{\tilde{\omega}}{h^{1/3}})d\tilde{\omega}d\tilde{\eta},

hence
F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=J(f_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}})
, where we have set

(5.8)

\hat{f}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{\eta},\frac{\tilde{\omega}}{h^{1/3}})=\chi_{1}(\frac{\tilde{\omega}}{\gamma_{2}|\tilde{\eta}|^{2/3}})e^{-iN_{2}L(\tilde{\omega})+it_{2}\tau_{q}(\tilde{\omega},\tilde{\eta})}\hat{g}_{h,(a,b)}(\tilde{\eta},\frac{\tilde{\omega}}{h^{1/3}}).

We turn to
\tilde{K}_{\omega,\gamma_{1}}(g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})})(t_{1},x,y)
which expands as

(5.9)

\int G(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi^{\#}(\omega)q^{1/6}(\eta)\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})e^{it_{1}\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)}\hat{g}_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(\eta,\frac{\omega}{h^{1/3}})d\eta.

Lemma 5.2
.

We have

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=J^{-1}(F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}})(y^{\prime},\rho).

Proof.

Recall from (
2.41
) that, in the variables
\theta=h\eta
,
\alpha=h^{2/3}\omega
, we may write

(5.10)

g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)=\frac{1}{h^{2d+1}}\int e^{\frac{i}{h}(y\cdot^{\prime}\theta^{\prime}-\Phi(\overline{x},\overline{y},\theta^{\prime},\alpha,s)+\rho\alpha+(\overline{x}-\tilde{x})\sigma+(\overline{y}-\tilde{y})\cdot\theta)}\\
\times q_{h}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\theta^{\prime},\alpha,s)q^{-1/6}(\theta^{\prime})\tilde{\chi}_{0}(\sigma)\varkappa(\theta)d\overline{x}d\overline{y}d\theta^{\prime}d\sigma dsd\alpha d\theta,

where
\tilde{\chi}_{0}
is supported near
0
and equals
1
on the support of
\chi_{0}
(of Lemma
2.11
). We thus obtain

(5.11)

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=\frac{1}{h^{2d+1}}\int e^{\frac{i}{h}(y^{\prime}\cdot\theta^{\prime}-\Phi(\overline{x},\overline{y},\theta^{\prime},\alpha,s)+\rho\alpha+\overline{x}\sigma+\overline{y}\cdot\theta)}q_{h}(\overline{x},\overline{y},\theta^{\prime},\alpha,s)q^{-1/6}(\theta^{\prime})\\
\times e^{-\frac{i}{h}(\tilde{x}\sigma+\tilde{y}.\theta)}\tilde{\chi}_{0}(\sigma)\varkappa(\theta)\hat{F}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\sigma/h,\theta/h).

As
\hat{F}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}=\tilde{\chi}_{0}(\sigma)\hat{F}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\sigma/h,\theta/h)
(which contains
\chi_{0}(\sigma)
), the last integral equals
J^{-1}(F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}})
.
∎

Using the lemma and
F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=J(f_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}})
yields

(5.12)

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)F_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=J^{-1}\circ J(f_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}})(y^{\prime},\rho)=f_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(y^{\prime},\rho)+O(h^{\infty}),

hence taking the Fourier transform of
g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)
(as needed in (
5.9
)) gives
\hat{f}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\theta/h,\alpha/h)
modulo
O(h^{\infty})
terms.
Hence,
J_{N_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y,\omega)
becomes

J_{N_{2},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2},x,y,\omega)=\int G(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi^{\#}(\omega)q^{1/6}(\eta)\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})e^{it_{1}\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)}\hat{f}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3}),

with
\hat{f}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}
given in (
5.8
). We find, modulo
O(h^{\infty})
terms from (
5.12
) (which stay
O(h^{\infty})
as the sums over
N_{1},N_{2}
below are finite),

(5.13)

\int_{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}}\mathcal{\tilde{P}}_{h,\gamma_{1}}(t_{1},x,y;{\tilde{x},\tilde{y}})\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma_{2}}(t_{2},\tilde{x},\tilde{y};a,b)\,d\tilde{x}d\tilde{y}=\sum_{N,\tilde{N}}\int e^{it_{1}\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)-iN_{1}L(\omega)}\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})G(x,y,\eta,\omega)\\
\times\chi^{\#}(\omega)q^{1/6}(\eta)\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})\hat{f}_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma_{2}}(\eta,\omega/h^{1/3})d\omega\\
=\sum_{N_{1},N_{2}}\int e^{i(t_{1}+t_{2})\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)-i(N_{1}+N_{2})L(\omega)}G(x,y,\eta,\omega)\chi^{\#}(\omega)q^{1/6}(\eta)\varkappa(h\eta)\varkappa(h\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta))\\
\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})\chi_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{2}|\eta|^{2/3}})\hat{g}_{h,(a,b)}(\eta,\frac{\omega}{h^{1/3}}).

Recall that
\tilde{\chi}_{1}=1
on the support of
\chi_{1}
. Therefore, if
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in 1/2^{\mathbb{N}}
, with
\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}
, then
\tilde{\chi}_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{1}|\eta|^{2/3}})\chi_{1}(\frac{\omega}{\gamma_{2}|\eta|^{2/3}})=0
and in this case we obtain (
5.7
).

It remains to deal with
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=:\gamma
: in this case, as
\tilde{\chi}_{1}\chi_{1}=\chi_{1}
, the last sum in (
5.13
) equals

(5.14)

\sum_{N_{1},N_{2}}<e^{-i(N_{1}+N_{2})L(\omega)},\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,(a,b),\gamma})(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y)>_{\omega}+O(h^{\infty}),

and we need to show that the double sum (over
N_{1},N_{2}
) yields indeed
\mathcal{P}_{h,\gamma}(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y;a,b)
. This is the goal of the next lemma.

Lemma 5.3
.

The sum in (
5.14
) can be written as

(5.15)

\sum_{N}<e^{-iNL(\omega)},\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,(a,b),\gamma})(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y)>_{\omega}.

Proof.

We use arguments similar to those in Lemma
2.16
, i.e. we check when the phase function
of
\int e^{-i(N_{1}+N_{2})L(\omega)}K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,(a,b),\gamma})(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y)d\omega
may be stationary with respect to
\alpha=h^{2/3}\omega
.

Recall first that, independently of the size of
a
(or
\tilde{x}
), the phase function of
\hat{g}_{h,(a,b)}
(or of
\hat{g}_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}
) equals that of
G(a,b,\eta,\omega)
(or of
G(\tilde{x},\tilde{y},\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})
) : this follows from Propositions
2.12
and and
2.35
in case
a>h^{2/3-\epsilon}
and from Lemma
2.35
when
a\lesssim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
and
\omega>h^{-\epsilon}
(for some
\epsilon>0
). (This property also holds when
a\lesssim h^{2/3-\epsilon}
and
\omega
is bounded). These constructions of
g_{h,(a,b)}(y^{\prime},\rho)
(resp.
g_{h,(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})}(y^{\prime},\rho)
) in both cases are very similar and these functions are compactly supported for
\rho
near
0
.
As in Lemma
2.16
, the phase function of
<e^{-iNL(\omega)},\chi^{\flat}(h^{2/3}\omega/\epsilon_{0})K_{\omega,\gamma}(g_{h,(a,b),\gamma})(t_{1}+t_{2},x,y)>_{\omega}
(with
\eta=\theta/h
,
\omega=\alpha/h^{2/3}
) is given by (
2.59
) and its critical points with respect to
\alpha,\sigma
satisfy the equations (
2.60
) and (
2.66
). Therefore, taking
\alpha=\gamma A
,
\sigma=\sqrt{\gamma}\Sigma
,
T_{1}=t_{1}/(2\sqrt{\gamma})
,
X=x/\gamma
we must have

T_{1}-\Sigma=\rho+2N_{1}\sqrt{A},\text{ where }\Sigma^{2}+Xq^{1/3}(\theta)(1+\ell+O(\gamma))=A\text{ and }\rho\text{ is small}.

If there are two non-trivial contributions for the same
t_{1}>0
, corresponding to
N_{1}
and
N_{1}-1
, then there exists
A_{1}\sim 1
and
\Sigma_{1}
satisfying
\Sigma^{2}_{1}+Xq^{1/3}(\theta)(1+\ell+O(\gamma))=A_{1}
such that

T_{1}-\Sigma_{1}=\rho+2(N_{1}-1)\sqrt{A_{1}}=\rho+2N_{1}\sqrt{A_{1}}-2\sqrt{A_{1}}.

The only solutions satisfying the last equations verify
\Sigma/\sqrt{A}\sim-1
and
\Sigma_{1}/\sqrt{A_{1}}\sim 1
. On the other hand, there are no solutions if, instead of
N_{1}-1
and
N_{1}
we consider
N_{1}-2
,
N_{1}
. Therefore, each time that we obtain non-trivial contributions from two consecutive values of
N_{1}
in the sum (
5.14
), the corresponding critical points in
\Sigma
satisfy
\Sigma^{2}/A\sim 1
. In the same way, if, for fixed
t_{2}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{2}+1
provide nontrivial contributions in
f_{N_{2},t_{2},\gamma}(y^{\prime},\rho)
and
f_{N_{2}+1,t_{2},\gamma}(y^{\prime},\rho)
, then the (normalized) Airy variable
\tilde{\Sigma}
in
G(a,b,\tilde{\eta},\tilde{\omega})
has to satisfy
\tilde{\Sigma}^{2}/\tilde{A}\sim 1
.

We introduce
\chi
, supported in
[-3/4,3/4]
and equal to
1
on
[-1/2,1/2]
and
\chi_{\pm}(z):=(1-\chi)(z)
if
\pm z>0
, and equal to
0
otherwise: then
\chi+\sum_{\pm}\chi_{\pm}=1
everywhere. If
\sigma
,
s
denote the Airy variables of
G(x,y,\eta,\omega)
and
G(a,b,\eta,\omega)
, we split its symbol in two parts,
p_{h}=p_{h}\chi(\sigma^{2}/\alpha)+p_{h}(1-\chi(\sigma^{2}/\alpha))
and write
K_{\omega,\gamma}(f)=K^{0}_{\omega,\gamma}(f)+K^{\pm}_{\omega,\gamma}(f)
. We then do the same with
g_{h,(a,b),\gamma}
, that we write as a sum
g_{h,(a,b),\gamma}=g^{0}_{h,(a,b),\gamma}+g^{\pm}_{h,(a,b),\gamma}
, where in the integral form of
\hat{g}_{h,(a,b),\gamma}
we added the cut-offs
\chi(s^{2}/\alpha)
and
(1-\chi)(s^{2}/\alpha)
.

Using the same arguments as above, we notice that, with
N_{t}=[t/4\sqrt{\gamma}]
, the only non-trivial contribution in last line of (
5.13
) comes from pairs
(N_{t},N_{\tilde{t}}),(N_{t}\mp 1,N_{\tilde{t}}),(N_{t},N_{\tilde{t}}\pm 1),(N_{t}\mp 1,N_{\tilde{t}}\pm 1)
which corresponds to the following products of cutoffs (with respect to
(\sigma/\alpha),(s/\alpha)
) :
(\chi,\chi)
,
(\chi_{\pm},\chi)
,
(\chi,\chi_{\pm})
and
(\chi_{\pm},\chi_{\pm})
. Summing up all these contributions allows to obtain (
5.15
) (as we recover
(\chi+\sum_{\pm}\chi_{\pm})(\cdot)(\chi+\sum_{\pm}\chi_{\pm})(\cdot)
); the sum of the contributions coming from
|N-N_{t}|\geq 2
or
|\tilde{N}-\tilde{N}_{\tilde{t}}|\geq 2
equals
O(h^{\infty})
by repeated integrations by parts and using that these sums stay finite. This achieves the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

From the last Lemma we conclude the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

6.
Appendix

We provide details on the proof of Lemma
2.28
and on obtaining
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}
(from Proposition
2.4
).

6.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.28

We start with twisted modes
\tilde{e}
(see Definition
2.24
):

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
k
such that
\omega_{k}h^{2/3}\leq\epsilon_{0}
with
\epsilon_{0}<1/100
. There exists constants
l_{0}>0
and
0<c_{0}<C_{0}
independent of
k,h,a
such that, for all
y
such that
|y|\leq l_{0}
,

(6.1)

c_{0}\leq\|\tilde{e}(\cdot,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{0}\,.

Proof.

By definition,
\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})=\frac{q(\eta)^{\frac{1}{6}}}{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}e^{-i(y\cdot\eta+|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))}G(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
and, using (
2.29
),
L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})=2\pi\|{Ai}(\cdot-\omega_{k})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\sim\omega_{k}^{1/2}
, for all
k\geq 1
. From (
2.8
), with
e_{0}
,
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
from Theorem
2.1
,

(6.2)

\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})=\frac{q(\eta)^{1/6}}{\sqrt{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}}e^{i(\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})-y\cdot\eta-|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|))}\Big(p_{0}{Ai}((-\zeta)+ip_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big),

where
\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})=-\omega_{k}+x|\eta|^{2/3}e_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})
.
Using Cauchy-Schwarz,

(6.3)

\int_{0}^{\infty}|\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})|^{2}dx\leq\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\Big[\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx+2\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}\\
{}\times\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big]\,.

For values
x|\eta|^{2/3}e_{0}>\omega_{k}
both
{Ai}(-\zeta)
and
{Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)
are exponentially decreasing.
Setting
X:=xe_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})
yields
x=x(X,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})
and

\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx=\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|\tilde{p}_{0}{Ai}(X|\eta|^{2/3}-\omega_{k})\Big|^{2}\frac{dx}{dX}dX,

where
\tilde{p}_{0}(X,y,\eta,\omega_{k}):=p_{0}(x(X,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})
. Here
\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3}\ll 1
from
\eta\sim 1/h
and
h^{2/3}\omega_{k}\ll 1
. Moreover
\frac{dx}{dX}=\frac{1}{e_{0}(0,\cdot)}+O(X)
,
e(0,\cdot)
is close to
1
and
X\sim x\leq\omega_{k}|\eta|^{2/3}\ll 1
(as for large
X
,
{Ai}(\cdot)
is exponentially decreasing.) With
C:=\sup|\tilde{p}_{0}|^{2}|\frac{dx}{dX}|<\infty
and rescaling variables
\tilde{X}=X|\eta|^{2/3}
,

(6.4)

\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\leq C\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)/|\eta|^{2/3}}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\omega_{k}}{Ai}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})^{2}dX=Cq^{1/3}(\eta/|\eta|).

For the integral with
{Ai}^{\prime}
we proceed similarly:
p_{1}
is bounded,
\|{Ai}^{\prime}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{\tilde{X}}}\lesssim\omega_{k}^{3/2}
and
\tilde{X}\lesssim\omega_{k}
as for large values we have exponential decay. We compute, with
X=xe_{0}(x,\cdot)
, then
\tilde{X}=X|\eta|^{2/3}
,

(6.5)

\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}|X\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(X|\eta|^{2/3}-\omega_{k})|^{2}dX\\
\leq\frac{C^{\prime}|\eta|^{-2/3}}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\omega_{k}}\tilde{X}^{2}|\eta|^{-4/3}|{Ai}^{\prime}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})|^{2}d\tilde{X}\leq C^{\prime}|\eta|^{-2}\omega_{k}^{3+1/2}/L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})\sim C^{\prime}(\omega_{k}h^{2/3})^{3}\ll C^{\prime},

where we used
|{Ai}^{\prime}(z)^{2}|\leq(1+|z|)^{1/2}
and that
p_{1}|_{x=0}=0
. Collecting all bounds yields the upper bound in (
6.1
), which will be enough for proving Lemma
2.28
below. We now prove the lower bound, which was used for the proof of Proposition
2.25
.
From (
6.3
), using (
6.4
), (
6.5
) and
\omega_{k}h^{2/3}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we have

(6.6)

\int_{0}^{\infty}|\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})|^{2}dx\geq\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\Big[\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx-2\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{}\times\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|xp_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big)^{1/2}-\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|xp_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\Big]\\
\geq\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx-O(\omega_{k}h^{2/3})^{3/2}\,.

As
\omega_{k}h^{2/3}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we are left to prove that
\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx
can be bounded from below by a constant independently of
k,h,a
. From ellipticity of
p_{0}\sim 1
, there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that
p_{0}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})\geq 1/2
for all
(x,y)
such that
|(x,y)|\leq\varepsilon_{1}
. On the other hand, for values
x|\eta|^{2/3}e_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})>\omega_{k}
with
|\eta|\sim 1/h
,
{Ai}(-\zeta)
is exponentially decreasing: thus, the bulk of the
L^{2}
norm of
p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)
is located for
x\lesssim\omega_{k}h^{2/3}\leq\epsilon_{0}\ll 1
and
\int_{4\epsilon_{0}}^{\infty}|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)|^{2}dx=O(h^{\infty})
. Taking
\epsilon_{0}
smaller if necessary such that
\epsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{1}/4
, we have, for all
|y|\leq\varepsilon_{1}

(6.7)

\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big|p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)\Big|^{2}dx\geq\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{4L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{4\epsilon_{0}}|{Ai}(-\zeta)|^{2}dx+O(h^{\infty})\,.

Ellipticity of
e_{0}
near
(x,y)=(0,0)
provides
c>0
and
\varepsilon_{2}>0
such that
e_{0}(x,y,\cdot)\geq c
for all
|(x,y)|\leq\varepsilon_{2}
. Taken
\varepsilon_{0}
smaller if necessary (so that
\varepsilon_{0}<\varepsilon_{2}/4
), we can assume that
e_{0}(x,y,\cdot)\geq c
for all
x\leq 2\varepsilon_{0}
and all
|y|\leq\varepsilon_{2}
. Let
X=xe_{0}(x,y,\eta/|\eta|,\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3})
for
x\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
|y|\leq l_{0}:=\min\{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\}
, then
|\frac{dx}{dX}|\geq\frac{1}{c}
for all
0\leq x\leq 4\varepsilon_{0}
and
|y|\leq l_{0}
and

\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta)}{4L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{4\epsilon_{0}}|{Ai}(-\zeta)|^{2}dx\geq\frac{q^{1/3}(\eta/|\eta|)}{4cL^{\prime}(\omega_{k})}\int_{0}^{4\epsilon_{0}|\eta|^{2/3}}{Ai}^{2}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})d\tilde{X}.

As
\omega_{k}\leq 4\varepsilon_{0}|\eta|^{2/3}
for
h|\eta|\in[1/2,2]
and
\omega_{k}h^{2/3}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, we find

(6.8)

\int_{0}^{4\epsilon_{0}|\eta|^{2/3}}{Ai}^{2}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})d\tilde{X}=\int_{0}^{\infty}{Ai}^{2}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})d\tilde{X}-\int_{4\epsilon_{0}|\eta|^{2/3}}^{\infty}{Ai}^{2}(\tilde{X}-\omega_{k})d\tilde{X}=L^{\prime}(\omega_{k})+O(h^{\infty})

and therefore the left hand side term in (
6.7
) is bounded from below by
\inf_{\Theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-2}}q^{1/3}(\Theta)/(4c)
for all
y
with
|y|\leq l_{0}
. As
q
is positive definite, this completes the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
∎

Proof.

(of Lemma
2.28
)
Using
B_{2j}(y,\cdot)=O(y)
for all
j\geq 2
and Corollary
6.10
, the phase
\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})-y\cdot\eta-|\eta|B_{0}(y,\eta/|\eta|)
of
\tilde{e}(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
reads (see (
6.2
))

(6.9)

(\tau_{q}(\omega_{k},\eta)-|\eta|)(B_{0}+B_{2})+\tau_{q}(\omega_{k},\eta)(O(x)\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}+O(\zeta/|\eta|^{2/3})\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}+O(y)\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}).

Using Lemma
6.1
,
\|\tilde{e}(.,\omega_{k})\|_{L^{2}(x\geq 0)}\lesssim 1
. From
\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)-|\eta|=\frac{\omega q^{2/3}(\eta)}{|\eta|+\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)}\sim\omega|\eta|^{1/3}
and
x\lesssim\omega_{k}/|\eta|^{2/3}
, taking derivatives with respect to
y
or
\eta
of the phase of (
6.2
) provides, at each step,
\omega_{k}|\eta|^{1/3}\sim\omega_{k}/h^{1/3}
. On the other hand, taking the derivatives (with respect to
y
or
\eta
) in the last factor of the right hand side of (
6.2
) and using that
{Ai}^{\prime\prime}(-\zeta)=\zeta{Ai}(-\zeta)
provides

(6.10)

\partial^{\beta_{1}}_{y}\partial^{\beta_{2}}_{\eta}\Big(p_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)+ip_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big)=(\omega|\eta|^{1/3})^{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}\Big(p^{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}_{0}{Ai}(-\zeta)+ip^{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}_{1}q^{-1/6}(\eta){Ai}^{\prime}(-\zeta)\Big),

where
p^{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}_{0}
and
p^{(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}_{1}
are asymptotic expansions with main contributions homogeneous of degree
0
and small parameter
(\omega_{k}|\eta|^{1/3})^{-1}
. Then, (
2.107
) follows from bounds like in (
6.4
) and (
6.5
).
∎

6.2.
The generating function
\varphi_{\Gamma}
of
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}

We aim at proving (
2.12
).
Set
\Theta=\varrho\vartheta
with
\varrho=|\Theta|
near
1
and
\vartheta=\Theta/|\Theta|
. Functions
A_{\Gamma},B_{\Gamma}
are to be defined near the glancing set
\mathcal{GL}=\{x=0,\Xi=0,\varrho-1=0\}
and for
(y,\vartheta)
near
\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. We work with formal Taylor expansions
F
near
\mathcal{GL}
such that
F=\sum_{a,b,c}f_{a,b,c}(y,\vartheta){X_{M}}^{a}(\varrho-1)^{b}\xi^{c}
.
We attribute a degree to each factor
x,\varrho-1,\Xi
: a monomial of the form
x^{a}(\varrho-1)^{b}\Xi^{c}
is homogeneous of degree
k
if and only if
c+2(a+b)=k
.
For such a formal serie
F(x,y,\Xi,\varrho,\vartheta)
, defined near
\mathcal{GL}
, we write
F=\sum_{k\geq 0}F_{k}
, where
F_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
k
; we also write
F\in\mathcal{H}_{\geq j}
if and only if
F=\sum_{k\geq j}F_{k}
. Therefore,
F_{0}=f_{0}(y,\vartheta)
,
F_{1}=\Xi f_{1}(y,\vartheta)
,
F_{2}={X_{M}}f_{2}^{0}(y,\vartheta)+(\varrho-1)f_{2}^{1}(y,\vartheta)+\Xi^{2}f_{2}^{2}(y,\vartheta)
, and so on. Replacing
{X_{M}},\xi,\eta
by their formulas (
2.13
) (as functions of
(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)
) and using that from (
2.11
)

(6.11)

\xi^{2}+R(x,y,\eta)=1\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad\Xi^{2}+|\Theta|^{2}+{X_{M}}q(\Theta)=1,

(where we notice that there is no
Y_{M}
in the second equation), we get

(6.12)

B_{\Gamma}=\sum_{j\geq 0}(\varrho-1)^{j}B_{2j}(y,\vartheta)\,,\quad A_{\Gamma}=\sum_{k\geq 1}A_{k}\,.

Using the third equation from (
2.13
) and
\xi|_{\mathcal{GL}}=0
, we have
A_{0}=0
. We also have
\xi(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)\in\mathcal{H}_{\geq 1}
and
{X_{M}}(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)\in\mathcal{H}_{\geq 2}
. Moreover, from the proof of Melrose’s theorem
[
15
]
, if formal series of the form (
6.12
) satisfy (
2.13
) and (
6.11
), then there exist
C^{\infty}
functions
A_{\Gamma},B_{\Gamma}
with the same Taylor development near
\mathcal{GL}
satisfying (
2.13
) and (
6.11
).

We first consider in (
6.11
) homogeneous terms of order
\leq 5
in the expansion of
\Gamma
: using
{X_{M}}(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)\in\mathcal{H}_{\geq 2}
, we are to write the explicit form of
A_{\Gamma}
up to
\mathcal{H}_{j\leq 3}
and
B_{\Gamma}
up to
\mathcal{H}_{j\leq 5}
. Let

A_{1}=\Xi\ell(Y,\vartheta)
,
A_{2}=\alpha(y,\vartheta)x+\beta(y,\vartheta)(\varrho-1)+\mu(y,\vartheta)\Xi^{2}
,
A_{3}=\alpha_{1}(y,\vartheta)x\Xi+\beta_{1}(y,\vartheta)(\varrho-1)\Xi+\mu_{1}(y,\vartheta)\Xi^{3}
and

A_{\Gamma}=A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Understanding this process will allow us to proceed with the expansion at any order. Observe that
A_{2j}
and
A_{2j+1}
always have the same number of terms: the only way to obtain homogeneous terms of order
2j+1
is to add a factor
\Xi
to homogeneous terms of order
2j
. Moreover, each
A_{2j}
and
A_{2j+1}
will have
2j+1
terms. This is of importance to understand why all
A_{k}
may be obtained from the system of equations that will follow below.

Using these expansions for
A_{\Gamma}
and
B_{\Gamma}
, and omitting variables for the functions
\ell
,
\mu
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\mu
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\beta_{1}
and
\mu_{1}
, (
2.13
) yields

(6.13)

\displaystyle X_{M}=

\displaystyle x(1+\ell+2\mu\Xi+\alpha_{1}x+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)+3\mu_{1}\Xi^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3})

(6.14)

\displaystyle\xi=

\displaystyle(1+\ell)\Xi+(2\alpha x+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2})+[2\alpha_{1}x\Xi+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)\Xi+\mu_{1}\Xi^{3}]+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4}

\displaystyle\eta=

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0}+(\varrho-1)(\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{2})+x\Xi\nabla_{y}\ell+x^{2}\nabla_{y}\alpha+x(\varrho-1)\nabla_{y}\beta\\
{}+x\Xi^{2}\nabla_{y}\mu+(\varrho-1)^{2}\nabla_{y}B_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}\,.\end{multlined}

Using
x\in\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}
we rewrite
x=x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}
where
x_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
; in the same way,
\eta=\eta_{0}+\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\eta_{3}+\eta_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}
, with
\eta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
. From (
6.2
) we obtain

(6.17)

\eta_{0}=\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0},\quad\eta_{1}=0,\quad\eta_{2}=(\varrho-1)(\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{2}).

Then, from
x\in\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}
,
\eta_{3}
is homogeneous of order
3
and
\eta_{3}=x_{2}\Xi\nabla_{y}\ell
, while

\eta_{4}=x_{3}\Xi\nabla_{y}\ell+x_{2}^{2}\nabla_{y}\alpha+x_{2}(\varrho-1)\nabla_{y}\beta+x_{2}\Xi^{2}\nabla_{y}\mu+(\varrho-1)^{2}\nabla_{y}B_{4}\,.

Similarly,
\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4}
,
\xi_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{j}
, depending on
x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
(notice that
\xi_{0}=0
). For
\xi
, an expansion up to
\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}
is sufficient as we work with
\xi^{2}
. From (
6.14
),

\xi_{1}=(1+\ell)\Xi\,,\quad\xi_{2}=2\alpha x_{2}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2}\,,\quad\xi_{3}=2\alpha x_{3}+2\alpha_{1}x_{2}\Xi+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)\Xi+\mu_{1}\Xi^{3}\,.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{L}:=\{(x,y,\vartheta,\varrho,\Xi),{X_{M}}q(\Theta)=1-\Xi^{2}-|\Theta|^{2}\}
, where
X_{M}=X_{M}(x,y,\vartheta,\varrho,\Xi)
is given by (
6.13
),
\Theta=\varrho\vartheta
.
If
(x,y,\vartheta,\varrho,\Xi)\in\mathcal{L}
then
x=x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}
, with

(6.18)

\displaystyle x_{2}=-\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\,,\quad x_{3}=\frac{2\mu\Xi(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}\,,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\,\,x_{4}=\frac{1}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\Big[\Xi^{4}\Big(\frac{3\mu_{1}}{(1+\ell)}-\frac{4\mu^{2}}{(1+\ell)^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}\Big)

(6.20)

\displaystyle{}+(\varrho-1)^{2}\Big(\frac{2}{(1+\ell)}(\frac{-2\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}+3+\beta_{1})\Big)

(6.21)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{}+\Xi^{2}(\varrho-1)\Big(2+2\Big(\frac{3\mu_{1}}{(1+\ell)}-\frac{4\mu^{2}}{(1+\ell)^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}\Big)

(6.22)

\displaystyle{}+\frac{1}{(1+\ell)}(\beta_{1}-\frac{2\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)})\Big)\Big].

Proof.

Using
X_{M}q(\Theta)=1-\Xi^{2}-|\Theta|^{2}
,
q(\Theta)=\varrho^{2}q(\vartheta)
and (
6.13
) yields

(6.23)

x(1+\ell+2\mu\Xi+\alpha_{1}x+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)+3\mu_{1}\Xi^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3})\varrho^{2}q(\vartheta)=1-\varrho^{2}-\Xi^{2}.

which immediately provides
x
in terms of
(y,\vartheta)
,
\Xi
and
\varrho-1
up to
\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}
as follows

x|_{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{(1-\varrho^{2}-\Xi^{2})}{\varrho^{2}q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\Big(1-\frac{2\Xi\mu}{1+\ell}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}\Big)\,;

hence, using
1-\varrho^{2}=-2(\varrho-1)-(\varrho-1)^{2}
, we get (
6.18
).
In order to obtain homogeneous terms of order
4
we write
x|_{\mathcal{L}}=x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}

and replace
x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
in (
6.23
); using moreover
\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}}=1-2(\varrho-1)+3(\varrho-1)^{2}+O((\varrho-1)^{3})
,
we find

(6.24)

(x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5})(1+\ell+2\mu\Xi+\alpha_{1}x_{2}+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)+3\mu_{1}\Xi^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3})\\
=-\frac{1}{q(\vartheta)}(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1)+(\varrho-1)^{2})\times(1-2(\varrho-1)+3(\varrho-1)^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 6})\\
=-\frac{1}{q(\vartheta)}\Big((\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))-(\varrho-1)(2\Xi^{2}+3(\varrho-1))+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 6}\Big).

Identifying homogeneous terms of order
2,3,4
, we obtain (again)
x_{2},x_{3}
as well as
x_{4}
:

(6.25)

x_{4}(1+\ell)+2\mu\Xi x_{3}+(\alpha_{1}x_{2}+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)+3\mu_{1}\Xi^{2})x_{2}=\frac{1}{q(\vartheta)}(\varrho-1)(2\Xi^{2}+3(\varrho-1)),

which yields (
6.19
) by substitution. This completes the proof of Lemma
6.3
.
∎

We now replace (

) and (

) in (

x,\xi,\eta
in

as follows. First,

\xi^{2}=(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4})^{2}=(1+\ell)^{2}\Xi^{2}+2(1+\ell)\Xi(2\alpha x_{2}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2})\\
+2(1+\ell)\Xi(2\alpha x_{3}+2\alpha_{1}x_{2}\Xi+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)\Xi+\mu_{1}\Xi^{3})+(2\alpha x_{2}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2})^{2}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}\,.

\displaystyle 2(1+\ell)\Xi\Big(-2\alpha\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2}\Big)\in\mathcal{H}_{3}\,,

(6.29)

\displaystyle(\xi^{2})_{4}=

\displaystyle\Big(-2\alpha\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2}\Big)^{2}+2(1+\ell)\Xi

(6.30)

\displaystyle{}\times\Big(2\alpha\frac{2\mu\Xi(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}-2\alpha_{1}\Xi\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}+\beta_{1}(\varrho-1)\Xi+\mu_{1}\Xi^{3}\Big)\in\mathcal{H}_{4}

\displaystyle=

We do the same for

\eta_{3}=-\Xi\nabla_{y}\ell\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)},\,\eta_{4}=\begin{multlined}(\varrho-1)^{2}\nabla_{y}B_{4}-\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\Big(\Xi^{2}\nabla_{y}\mu+(\varrho-1)\nabla_{y}\beta\Big)\\
{}+\nabla_{y}\ell\frac{2\mu\Xi^{2}(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}+\nabla_{y}\alpha\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))^{2}}{q^{2}(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}\,.\end{multlined}

We expand
R(x,y,\eta)R_{0}(y,\eta)+xR_{1}(y,\eta)+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}R_{2}(y,\eta)+O(x^{3})
(

R(x,y,\eta)=R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+(\eta_{2}+\eta_{3}+\eta_{4})\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\nabla^{2}_{\eta,\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}\\
{}+(x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4})(R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})+\eta_{2}\nabla_{\eta}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0}))+\frac{1}{2}x_{2}^{2}R_{2}(y,\eta_{0})+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}\,.

We set
R(x,y,\eta)=(R)_{0}+(R)_{1}+(R)_{2}+(R)_{3}+(R)_{4}+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 5}
, with
(R)_{j}
the homogeneous term of order

in

; from (

) and (

) we get
(R)_{0}=R_{0}(y,\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0})
,
(R)_{1}=0
, and

(6.35)

\displaystyle(R)_{2}=

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+x_{2}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})\,,\quad\eta_{0}=\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0}(y,\vartheta)\,,

(6.36)

\displaystyle(R)_{3}=

\displaystyle\eta_{3}\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+x_{3}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})\,,

(6.37)

\displaystyle(R)_{4}=

\displaystyle\eta_{4}\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{2}^{2}\nabla^{2}_{\eta,\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+x_{2}\eta_{2}\nabla_{\eta}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})+\frac{1}{2}x_{2}^{2}R_{2}(y,\eta_{0})\,.

Recall from (
2.2
)
that we had set
R_{0}(y,\eta)=R(0,y,\eta)=|\eta|^{2}+O(y)
and
R_{1}(y,\eta)=\frac{\partial R}{\partial x}(0,y,\eta)
,
q(\eta):=R_{1}(0,\eta)
. From (
6.11
) it follows that for
(x,y,\vartheta,\varrho,\Xi)\in\mathcal{L}
we must have

(6.38)

\xi^{2}+R(x,y,\eta)=1\,.

On
\mathcal{L}
we have obtained
x|_{\mathcal{L}}
as a sum of homogeneous terms of the form (
6.18
), (
6.19
) which in turn has allowed to do the same for
\xi^{2}
and
R(x,y,\eta)
; it remains to get homogeneous terms of order
j
for
j\in\{0,2,3,4\}
in (
6.38
) to obtain a system of equations whose unknown are the coefficients of
A_{\Gamma}
and
B_{\Gamma}
(notice there are no terms of order
j=1
). First, we have

R_{0}(y,\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0})=1
,
(homogeneous terms of order
0
).
The next lemma easily follows from solving transport equations:

Lemma 6.4
.

For
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\setminus 0
, there exists an unique function
\phi(y,\eta)
, homogeneous of degree
1
in
\eta
, solving the eikonal equation
R_{0}(y,\nabla_{y}\phi)=|\eta|^{2}
, with
\phi_{|y\cdot\eta=0}=0
.

We then obtain
B_{0}
: from
R_{0}(y,\eta)=|\eta|^{2}+O(y)
, we have
\phi(y,\eta)=y\cdot\eta(1+O(y))
, and

(6.39)

B_{0}(y,\vartheta)=\phi(y,\vartheta)-y\cdot\vartheta\,.

As a consequence we have

B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
and
\nabla_{y}B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
. Back to (
6.38
), consider homogeneous terms of order
2
such that
(\xi^{2})_{2}+(R)_{2}=0
; using (
6.27
) and (
6.35
), this translates into

(6.40)

(1+\ell)^{2}\Xi^{2}+(\varrho-1)(\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{2})\cdot\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})-\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})=0\,.

We first match coefficients of
\Xi^{2}
in (
6.40
):

(1+\ell)^{2}=\frac{R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}

which yields
1+\ell=\Big(\frac{R_{1}(y,\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0})}{q(\vartheta)}\Big)^{1/3}
. As
R_{1}(0,\vartheta)=q(\vartheta)
and
\nabla_{y}B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
, we obtain
\ell(0,\vartheta)=0
. We now match coefficients of
\varrho-1
,

(6.41)

(\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{2})\cdot\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})=\frac{2R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\,,

which is a linear transport equation for
B_{2}(y,\vartheta)
and we can take
B_{2|y.\vartheta=0}=0
: at
y=0
, the transport field is
2\vartheta\cdot\nabla_{y}
, as
\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})=2\eta_{0}+O(y)
,
\eta_{0}=\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0}(y,\vartheta)
and
\nabla_{y}B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
.
The first three equations involving
B_{0},B_{2}
and
\ell
can be solved explicitly using only homogeneous contributions up to order
2
. We consider now homogeneous terms of order
3
in (
6.38
), i.e.
(\xi^{2})_{3}+(R)_{3}=0
. This yields, using (
6.28
) together with (
6.36
), (
6.33
) and (
6.18
)

(6.42)

2(1+\ell)\Xi\Big(-2\alpha\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}+\beta(\varrho-1)+\mu\Xi^{2}\Big)\\
-\Xi\frac{(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\nabla_{y}\ell\cdot\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})+\frac{2\mu\Xi(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})=0\,,

in which there are only
\Xi^{3}
and
\Xi(\varrho-1)
terms. Exactly like we did for (
6.40
), we match coefficients for these terms separately. Unknown functions are
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\mu
(we already chose
\ell
and
B_{0},B_{2}
.) Using that
q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{3}=R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})
, we get

(6.43)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}4\mu(1+\ell)=\frac{4\alpha}{q(\vartheta)}+\frac{\nabla_{y}\ell}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0}),\text{ terms in }\Xi^{3};\\
(2\mu+\beta)(1+\ell)=\frac{4\alpha}{q(\vartheta)}+\frac{\nabla_{y}\ell}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\cdot\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})\,,\text{ terms in }\Xi(\varrho-1).\end{array}\right.

The last system of two equations and three unknown functions implies
\beta=2\mu
, and provides a relation between
\alpha
and
\mu
(given by the first equation in (
6.43
)). Moreover, at
y=0
, we have
q(\vartheta)=R_{1}(0,\vartheta)
and
3\nabla_{y}\ell(0,\vartheta)=\frac{\nabla_{y}R_{1}(0,\vartheta)}{q(\vartheta)}
.
We summarize what we obtained so far:

Proposition 6.5
.

The phase function
\Gamma(x,\cdot)=B_{\Gamma}+xA_{\Gamma}
is such that, near the glancing set
\mathcal{GL}
,

(6.44)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}A_{\Gamma}(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)=\Xi\ell(y,\vartheta)+\alpha(y,\vartheta)x+\mu(y,\vartheta)(\Xi^{2}+|\Theta|^{2}-1)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}\,,\\
B_{\Gamma}(y,\Theta)=B_{0}(y,\vartheta)+(\varrho-1)B_{2}(y,\vartheta)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}\,,\end{array}\right.

where
\vartheta=\Theta/|\Theta|
,
\varrho=|\Theta|
and the functions
B_{0}

B_{2}
were defined in (
6.39
), (
6.41
). Morover,

\beta(y,\vartheta)=2\mu\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu(y,\vartheta)=\frac{\alpha}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}-\frac{\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{0})}{4q(\vartheta)}\cdot\nabla_{y}\Big(\frac{1}{1+\ell}\Big)\,.

We also have
\ell(0,\vartheta)=0
,
B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
,
\nabla_{y}B_{0}(0,\vartheta)=0
,
B_{2}(0,\vartheta)=0
and
\nabla_{y}B_{2}(0,\vartheta)=0
,
B_{0}
(resp.
B_{2}
) is homogeneous of order
1
(resp. of order
0
) in the second variable.

Remark 6.6
.

The restriction of
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}
to
\mathcal{GL}_{M}
is given by
x=\xi=0
and
Y_{M}=y+\nabla_{\Theta}B_{\Gamma}(y,\Theta)|_{\varrho=1}
,
\eta=\Theta+\nabla_{y}B_{0}(y,\Theta)(y,\Theta)|_{\varrho=1}
.
It preserves the canonical foliation:
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}\Big(\{Y_{M}=Y_{0}+2s\vartheta_{0},\Theta=\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{0}^{2}=1\}\Big)
is an integral curve of
H_{R_{0}}
on
R_{0}=1
.

To complete the proof of Proposition
2.4
, we are to identify homogeneous terms of order
at least
4
for
A_{\Gamma}
and
B_{\Gamma}
in (
6.11
). One gets a cascade of linear equations (similar to those obtained by identifying homogeneous terms of order
0,1,2,3

in order to chose
\alpha,\beta,\mu
) which may be solved by induction.
We only do it for homogeneous terms of order
4
in (
6.38
) as an example:
let
(\xi^{2})_{4}+(R)_{4}=0
and match coefficients for
\Xi^{4}
and
\Xi^{2}(\varrho-1)
using (
6.29
), (
6.37
), (
6.33
), (
6.17
), (
6.18
),

(6.45)

\displaystyle\frac{4\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)}-2(1+\ell)\mu_{1}=

\displaystyle\,9\mu^{2}+\frac{R_{2}(y,\eta_{0})}{2q^{2}(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}+\frac{\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\cdot\nabla_{y}\Big(\frac{\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})}{4q(\vartheta)}\cdot\nabla_{y}(\frac{1}{(1+\ell)})\Big)

(6.46)

\displaystyle\frac{4\alpha_{1}}{q(\vartheta)}-(1+\ell)\beta_{1}=

\displaystyle\,10\mu^{2}+\frac{R_{2}(y,\eta_{0})}{q^{2}(\vartheta)(1+\ell)^{2}}-\frac{\nabla_{\eta}R_{1}(y,\eta_{0})}{2q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\cdot(\vartheta+\nabla_{y}B_{2})

(6.47)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{}+\frac{2\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})}{q(\vartheta)(1+\ell)}\cdot\nabla_{y}\Big(\frac{\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0})}{4q(\vartheta)}\cdot\nabla_{y}(\frac{1}{(1+\ell)})\Big)\,,

where both RHSs only contain
\mu^{2}
and known function such as
\ell
,
B_{0}
,
B_{2}
,
R_{0}
,
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
.

Remark 6.7
.

If we require
\beta_{1}=2\mu_{1}
(which implies
A_{3}=\Xi(x\alpha_{1}+(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))\mu_{1})
), then by difference between the last two equations
\mu^{2}
is uniquely determined (and therefore
\alpha
using Proposition
6.5
). Similarly, we could ask for

A_{2j}=x\alpha_{j}+(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))\mu_{j},\quad\quad A_{2j+1}=x\Xi\alpha_{j-1}+(\Xi^{2}+2(\varrho-1))\Xi\mu_{j-1},\quad j\geq 2\,,

but this would determine
B_{2j}
in an unique way. Indeed, for
j=1
requiring
\beta_{1}=2\mu_{1}
provides a unique
\mu
and then a unique
\alpha
. Moreover, identifying the coefficients of
(\varrho-1)^{2}
in homogeneous terms of degree
4
in (
6.38
) does not involve
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\mu_{1}
, but only
\nabla_{y}B_{4}
(with
\nabla_{\eta}R_{0}(y,\eta_{0}\neq 0
) and
\alpha,\mu,\ell
(this is the first occurrence of
B_{4}
.) Indeed,
\eta_{4}
does not contain
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\mu_{1}
, which appear only in
(\xi^{2})_{4}
(with
\Xi^{4}
or
\Xi^{2}(\varrho-1)
). Therefore for given
\alpha,\mu
, this equation (obtained by identifying coefficients of
(\varrho-1)^{2}
) determines
\nabla_{y}B_{4}
(and therefore
\nabla_{y}B_{4}(0,\vartheta)\neq 0
unlike for
B_{0},B_{2}
.)

Remark 6.8
.

That the formal expansion is not uniquely defined reflects that the
group of canonical transformations which preserves the model
\{X_{M}=0,\Xi^{2}+|\Theta|^{2}+X_{M}q(\Theta)=1\}
is not trivial.

6.3.
Equivalence of phase functions for
G(x,y,\eta,\omega)

Both phases
\psi+s^{3}/3-s\zeta
(from (
2.8
) in Theorem
2.1
) and
y\cdot\eta+\sigma^{3}/3+\sigma(xq^{1/3}(\eta)-\omega)+\tau_{q}\Gamma(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\eta)/\tau_{q},\eta/\tau_{q})
(from (
2.14
)) define the same Lagrangian. We now explain how they are related. From a classical result (see
[
3
]
) on the normal form of integrals whose phases have degenerate critical points of order
2
, we have:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=\sigma^{3}/3+\sigma(xq^{1/3}(\theta)-\alpha)+x\tau_{q}A_{\Gamma}(x,y,\sigma q^{1/3}(\theta)/\tau_{q},\theta/\tau_{q})
. There exists a unique map
\sigma\rightarrow s
and
\Upsilon(x,y,\theta,\alpha)\in C^{\infty}
such that
\phi(x,y,\theta,\alpha,\sigma)=s^{3}/3-s\zeta(x,y,\theta,\alpha)+\Upsilon(x,y,\theta,\alpha)

and
\frac{ds}{d\sigma}\notin\{0,\infty\}
. Let
w:=(x,y,\theta,\alpha)
and denote
\sigma_{0}(w)
the unique solution to
\partial^{2}_{\sigma,\sigma}\phi(w,\sigma)=0
; then the two saddle points of
\phi
, that we denote
\sigma_{\pm}(w)
, correspond to the critical points
s_{\pm}(w):=\pm\sqrt{\zeta(w)}
and such that
\sigma_{\pm}(w)=\sigma_{0}(w)\pm\sqrt{\zeta(w)}k(\pm\sqrt{\zeta(w)},w)
, with
k(u,w)=1+\sum_{j\geq 1}k_{j}(w)u^{j}
, where
k_{j}
are smooth functions of
w
.
Moreover,

(6.48)

\frac{3}{4}\zeta^{3/2}(w)=\phi(w,\sigma_{-}(w))-\phi(w,\sigma_{+}(w)),\quad\Upsilon(w):=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\phi(w,\sigma_{+}(w))+\phi(w,\sigma_{-}(w))\Big)\,.

Corollary 6.10
.

We may write
\psi(x,y,\theta,\alpha)=y\cdot\theta+\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)B_{\Gamma}(y,\theta/\tau_{q})+\Upsilon(x,y,\theta,\alpha)
, with

(6.49)

\Upsilon(x,y,\theta,\alpha)=-x\big(x\mu(y,\theta/|\theta|)q(\theta)/\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)(1+\ell(y,\theta/|\theta|)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 4}\big)-k_{1}\zeta(\zeta+x\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}+\zeta\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2})\,.

Proof.

Compute
\sigma_{0}
: as
\partial^{2}_{\sigma,\sigma}\phi(w,\sigma)=2\sigma+2x\big(\mu(y,\theta/|\theta|)q^{2/3}(\theta)/\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 1}\big)=0
, where
\mathcal{H}_{j=1}
contains only multiples of
\sigma
, we get

\sigma_{0}(x,y,\theta,\alpha)=-x\big(\mu(y,\theta/|\theta|)q^{2/3}(\theta)/\tau_{q}(\alpha,\theta)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}\big)
,
where all
\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 2}
in the RHS come with weights
x
and
\alpha
. We develop
\phi(w,\sigma)
near
\sigma=0
and replace
\sigma
by
\sigma_{\pm}(w)
: as
\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{+}+\sigma_{-})=\sigma_{0}+k_{1}(w)\zeta(w)(1+O(\zeta(w)))
, using (
6.48
) yields (
6.49
). Moreover,
\psi
contains
B_{\Gamma}
as it does not depend on
\sigma
. ∎

7.
Index of notations

Below is a commented list of the main notations, with reference to their very first occurence.

7.1.
General notations (used consistently throughout the paper)

•

(\Omega,g)
=
d
dimensional manifold,
d\geq 2
,
\Delta_{g}
its Laplace Beltrami operator, section
1
.

•

\upgamma(d)
encodes the loss in Strichartz estimates w.r.t. the case without boundary, Theorem
1.5
.

•

(x,y)
, boundary normal coordinates;
t
the time variable; locally,
\Omega=\{(x,y):x>0,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\}
, section
2
.

•

(\xi,\eta,\tau)
, dual variables:
(x,y,t,\xi,\eta,\tau)\in T^{*}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{t})
.
For
(x,y)
near
(0,y)
, the metric is
\xi^{2}+R(x,y,\eta)
. In a neighborhood of
(0,0)\in\partial\Omega
,

R_{0}(y,\partial_{y}):=R(0,y,\eta)\,,\quad R_{1}(y,\eta):=\partial_{x}R(0,y,\partial)\,,

section
2
and (
2.2
).

•

\Delta_{M}=\partial^{2}_{x}+\sum_{j}\partial^{2}_{y_{j}}+x\sum_{j,k=1}^{d-1}R^{j,k}_{1}(0)\partial_{y_{j}}\partial_{y_{k}}
: model Laplace operator, (
2.3
); Multipliers

q(\eta)=\sum_{j,k=1}^{d-1}R^{j,k}_{1}(0)\eta_{j}\eta_{k},\quad\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta):=\sqrt{|\eta|^{2}+\omega q(\eta)^{2/3}}\,.

•

\{e_{k}(x,\eta)\}_{k\geq 0}
: in the spectral decomposition of
-\Delta_{M}
(section
2.3.1
), an explicit orthonormal base of eigenfunctions associated to eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}(\eta)
, where

\lambda_{k}(\eta)=|\eta|^{2}+\omega_{k}q(\eta)^{2/3}=\tau_{q}^{2}(\omega_{k},\eta).

•

\{-\omega_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
: zeros of the Airy function in decreasing order. Everywhere in the paper
\omega>1
and serves as a substitute to the
\xi
variable: if
Q_{y}
is the differential operator with symbol
q
,
\alpha=h^{2/3}\omega
quantizes the operator
x-Q_{y}^{-1}\partial_{x}^{2}
.

•

s
,
\sigma
: integration variables in Airy type oscillatory integrals, (
2.14
), (
2.5
).

•

(a,b)
coordinates of the source point, mostly set with
b=0
, Theorem
1.1
.

•

h\in(0,1)
(Theorem
1.1
),
\gamma\in(0,1)
with
1/\gamma\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
(Section
3.1
): small parameters.

•

\lambda=a^{3/2}/h
,
\lambda_{\gamma}=\gamma^{3/2}/h
: large parameters, Section
3.1
.

•

(X,Y,T)
, rescaled coordinates (using some combination of
a
,
h
or
\lambda
,
\lambda_{\gamma}
as rescaling parameters), Section
3.1
.

•

\Sigma
,
S
,
A
: rescaled variables in Airy-type oscillatory integrals, Section
3.1
.

•

\omega
: (
2.3
), parameter and integration variable, successively rescaled to
\alpha
((
2.21
)) and then
A
(Section
3.1
). Stationary phases in oscillatory integrals are performed with respect to
\alpha
,
\sigma
,
s
or their rescaled versions
A
,
\Sigma
,
S
, less frequently
\eta
, with a large parameter being
1/h
,
\lambda
or
\lambda_{\gamma}
.

•

\theta
: (Section
2
) rescaled
\eta
, near
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and
\rho=|\theta|
,
\vartheta=\theta/\rho
.

7.2.
Localisations in phase space

•

We localize
\tau_{q}(\omega,\eta)\sim 1/h
and
|\eta|\sim 1/h
.
For small
x
, this corresponds to large frequencies
-\Delta\sim-\Delta_{M}\sim 1/h^{2}
and ”tangent” directions: the number of reflections on the boundary may be quite large.

•

A further localization is to values
\omega/|\eta|^{2/3}\sim\gamma
. Informally, it relates to the angle of incidence at the boundary for a ray starting tangentially from
(\gamma,0)
.

•

Cut-offs :
\varkappa\geq 0
is a cut-off function in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
with
m=1
or with
m=d-1
, localizing around a small neighbourhood of
1
(for
d=1
), or near
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}
for
m=d-1
;
\varkappa_{1}
is a 1-d
\varkappa
. We also have
\chi^{\flat}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\chi^{\flat}=1
on
(-\infty,1]
and
\chi^{\flat}=0
on
[2,\infty)
and
\chi^{\sharp}=1-\chi^{\flat}
. Also,
\chi_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is supported is a small, fixed neighborhood of
0
.

7.3.
Operators, kernels and quasimodes

•

G(x,y,\eta,\omega)
: a quasimode, (
2.9
) or (
2.14
); satisfies (
2.9
)
-\Delta G=\tau_{q}^{2}G+O_{C^{\infty}}(\tau_{q}^{-\infty})
.

•

K_{\omega}(f)(t,x,y)
: operator related to wave flow (
2.31
), acting on smooth
f
.

•

J(f)(x,y)
: Fourier integral operator, (
2.24
).

•

\mathcal{P}_{h,a}(t,x,y)
, (
2.49
) and (
2.50
), our parametrix for the wave equation

•

V_{N}
: a wave in the expansion over
N
of
\mathcal{P}_{h,a}
, (
2.54
).

•

V_{N,\gamma}
: further localized with
\chi_{1}(\omega/(\gamma|\eta|^{2/3}))
, (
3.9
).

•

\mathcal{P}_{h,a,\gamma}
: the corresponding sum over
N
, (
3.12
).

•

\mathcal{E}_{M}(\cdot,\omega_{k})
: galery modes for the model Laplacian
\Delta_{M}
, (
2.3.1
).

•

e_{k}(x,\eta)
: eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{F}_{y}(\Delta_{M})
, (
2.82
).

•

e(x,y,\eta,\omega)
: quasimodes for
\Delta_{g}
, (
2.92
).

•

g_{h,a}
,
g_{h,a,j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
: functions to serve as arguments to
J
and
K_{\omega}
to construct a suitable smoothed out Dirac data, (
2.38
), Propositions
2.18
and
2.19
.

•

F_{\omega_{k}}(g)(x,y)
: operator acting on functions
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
, average (with density
\hat{g}(\eta)
) of quasimodes
e(x,y,\eta,\omega_{k})
, (
2.93
).

•

\mathfrak{L}(f)(y)
: operator actiong on
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
which allows to ”get rid” of the term
B_{0}
in the phase of
e(x,y,\eta,\omega)
, (
2.94
).

•

\tilde{F}_{\omega_{k}}(f)(x,y)=F_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathfrak{L}(f)(x,y)
: its main property is that it can be inverted.

7.4.
Phase functions and canonical transformation

•

\zeta(x,y,\eta,\omega)
,
\psi(x,y,\eta,\omega)
: the phase functions of
G(x,y,\eta,\omega)
from Theorem
2.1
.

•

\Sigma_{0}
: (
2.10
), a neighborhood of a glancing point in the model case.

•

\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}
, (
2.11
),the canonical transformation defined in a conic neighborhood of
\Sigma_{0}
mapping the model case (variables
(X_{M},Y_{M},\Xi,\Theta)
) to the general case (variables
(x,y,\xi,\eta)
).

•

\varphi_{\Gamma}(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)=x\Xi+y\Theta+\Gamma(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)
: Proposition
2.4
, the generating function for
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{M}
, with
\Gamma(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)=B_{\Gamma}(y,\Theta)+xA_{\Gamma}(x,y,\Xi,\Theta)
from (
2.12
).

•

A_{\Gamma}
,
B_{\Gamma}
: phase functions that are formal series (
6.12
) from Section
6.2
, defined near
\mathcal{GL}=\{x=0,\Xi=0,\varrho-1=0\}
and for
(y,\vartheta)
near
\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, where
\Theta=\varrho\vartheta
. Their explicit form is given in (
6.44
).

•

\mathcal{H}_{\geq j}=\{F\text{ such that }F=\sum_{k\geq j}F_{k},\text{ with }F_{k}\text{ homogeneous of degree }k\geq 1\}
, where a monomial of the form
x^{a}(\varrho-1)^{b}\Xi^{c}
is homogeneous of degree
k
if
c+2(a+b)=k
.

•

\ell(y,\vartheta)
(which defines
A_{1}=\Xi\ell
),
\alpha(y,\vartheta)
,
\beta(y,\vartheta)
,
\mu(y,\vartheta)
(which define
A_{2}=\alpha(y,\vartheta)x+\beta(y,\vartheta)(\varrho-1)+\mu(y,\vartheta)\Xi^{2}
) so that
A_{\Gamma}=A_{1}+A_{2}+\mathcal{H}_{\geq 3}
: other functions related to
\Gamma
from Section
6.2
.

•

B_{\Gamma}(y,\Theta)=B_{0}(y,\vartheta)+(\varrho-1)B_{2}(y,\vartheta)+\mathcal{H}_{j\geq 3}
, whose properties are stated in Proposition
6.5
.

•

\mathcal{L}=\{(x,y,\vartheta,\varrho,\Xi),p_{M,2}(X_{M},Y_{M},\Xi,\Theta)=0\}
defined in Lemma
6.3
.

•

W\!F_{{h}}
, the semiclassical wavefront set (see
[
25
]
).

•

\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
: the phase function of
V_{N,\gamma}
defined in (
3.15
).

•

\Phi^{M}_{N,a,\gamma}
: the phase function in the model case
(\Omega,\Delta_{M})
. (In general, a notation with an additional
M
indicates that we consider the model situation.)

•

\tilde{\Phi}_{N,a,\gamma}
, a rescaled
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}
(see (
3.48
)) with
\sigma=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}\Sigma
,
\alpha=\gamma|\theta|^{2/3}A
,
s=\sqrt{\gamma}|\theta|^{1/3}S
.

•

\Psi_{N,a,\gamma}
: relabeled
\tilde{\Phi}_{N,a,\gamma}
after rescaling
x=\gamma X
,
t=\sqrt{\gamma}T
,
y=\sqrt{\gamma}Y
.

•

\mathcal{N}(t,x,y)
: the set of
N
with significant contributions of the phase
\Phi_{N,a,\gamma}(t,x,y,\cdot)
, defined in (
3.14
).

•

\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}
: a cylinder defined by (
3.21
).

•

\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d}
: enlargement of
\mathcal{N}
defined by
\mathcal{N}^{1}_{d}(t,x,y)=\cup_{\mathcal{C}_{\gamma}(t,x,y)}\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
in (
3.20
).

•

\tilde{\Gamma}_{\gamma}
,
\breve{\Gamma}_{\gamma}
(see (
3.61
)),
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
(see Lemma
3.8
),
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
(see Lemma
3.10
): remainder terms in phase functions (they do not influence the behaviour of the corresponding phase functions.)
